V ARIANCE 
 
Any variance to this manual or the policies herein must be approved by the Area 
Manager or higher. The variance must be documented using the Variance 
Request form ( GL-PCP-OEPS-L4-64). Once complete, a copy will be forwarded 
to the Global Service Line Manager. 
 
If the variance requires a safe work permit, that permit must also be completed 
as part of the planning process. 
 
For any variances that may affect the safety of any personnel, the Area Manager 
must confer with the District HSSE Representative or higher. 
 
In no case shall a variance be issued that would contravene regulatory or 
contractual obligations. 
 
 
CONTROLLED DOCUMENT 
 
This manual, although intranet based, is set out so that it can be printed. 
However, only the online version is controlled and all other formats must be 
checked against the online version for validity. 
 
Navigation through the manual is through the Table of Contents and appropriate 
hyperlinks. A search engine is also accessible to empower users to search b y 
topic.

1 CONDUCT AND PROFESSIONALISM 
 
When working with other members of industry it is important to remember that 
each of our behaviors and actions have an impact on how Weatherford is 
perceived by others. For this reason it is very important that all Field Service 
personnel make every effort  to conduct themselves professionally and 
courteously at all times. The ability to work professionally with other Field Service 
personnel and representatives of our customers will dramatically improve the 
quality of the working environment for everyone involved.  
 
The way our equipment is presented to the public has a significant impact on our 
image and perception of quality. Field Service employees are required at all 
times to maintain clean and well-functioning 
 equipment as well as ensuring that they the mselves are tidy and well presented. 
This means that regular preventive maintenance must be performed on the 
equipment and deficiencies must be addressed as quickly as possible. 
Equipment should be cleaned as required to maintain a polished appearance. 
The more often the equipment is cleaned, the less cleaning time will be required 
so regular effort should be made to maintain the tidiness of the unit. 
 
Our personal appearance is also very important. The following guidelines should 
always be observed: 
 
 Field Service personnel must be clean shaven and generally well groomed 
 Coveralls must be clean, in good condition and meet local WFT 
requirements 
 Hard hats must be clean, should be worn in the intended orientation 
and meet local WFT requirements 
 Proper eye and foot protection must always be used 
 Any other WFT, local or customer PPE requirements must be met 
 
Each employee’s conduct and professionalism are considered part of the 
competency requirement for operating COROD mobile equipment and will be 
considered when assessing each operator’s performance.

2 INTRODUCTION 
 
This manual provides a thorough description of the general operation of a 
COROD Continuous Sucker Rod Mobile Gripper unit. The purpose of this manual 
is to familiarize operators of the COROD Mobile Gripper with its features and to 
outline the basic jobs performed by the Mobile Gripper . Information on the 
operation of the carrier vehicle should be obtained from the original manufacturer 
of the vehicle.  
 
Throughout this manual you will see the use of the term COROD. This term 
represents COROD’s Registered Trademark for the Continu ous Sucker Rod it 
manufactures. When reference is made regarding the company itself, the name 
COROD will be used in capital letters. 
 
Also included with this manual are two publications outlining basic  maintenance 
and inspection  in enough detail to enable the operator to perform basic 
preventive maintenance of the deck and chassis  as well as the various 
scheduled inspections. 
 
The procedures outlined in this manual are intended as guideli nes to be used 
where practical. Please note that the warnings used in this manual  to describe 
procedures which can be damaging to the equipment or personnel using the  
equipment, are not exhaustive.  COROD could not possibly make such a broad 
evaluation of all the possible methods that could be d evised to operate its 
equipment. Therefore, COROD has not made any such evaluation.

3 GENERAL INFORMATION 
3.1 HYDRAULIC SYSTEMS 
3.1.1 Basics of Hydraulic Systems  
 
Hydraulics is a method of transmitting power from one place to another using an 
incompressible fluid. Most often this fluid would be oil engineered for hydraulic 
use. With hydraulics, the amount of force applied at one end can be greatly 
multiplied at the other end. The catch to hydraulic power transmission is that you 
are trading for ce for distance. In the example below, cylinder B is three times 
larger in diameter than cylinder A. If cylinder A is moved with a certain amount of 
force, cylinder B will see nine times the force that had been applied to cylinder A. 
To achieve this howeve r the fluid in cylinder A would have to travel nine times 
further than the fluid in cylinder B would be moved. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The hydraulic system of a COROD Mobile Gripper is very complex but the basic 
idea remains the same. When a lever is pulled, fluid power is transmitted through 
the high pressure lines to the component that is to be actuated.

3.1.2 Maintenance and Cleanliness of the Hydraulic System 
 
A properly operated and cared for hydraulic system involves routine 
maintenance. This consists of changing or cleaning filters, strainers, and 
changing or adding the system fluid. An improperly operated and maintained 
system will eventually result in severe and expensive damage through excessive 
pressure, overheating and contamination.  
 
A carefully planned routine maintenance schedule can drastically reduce 
hydraulic problems. Ordinary problems can be eliminated and out of the ordinary 
problems can be anti cipated and averted before costly and time consuming 
equipment failure occurs. 
 
When working on hydraulic systems, cleanliness should be the first concern. An 
estimated 80% of all hydraulic system failures can be traced back to 
contamination in the fluid. Dirt and metal particles in the system can clog 
openings, damage valves and cause pumps to seize. Removal of hydraulic 
components or disconnection of hoses should only be carried out in a clean, 
particle free area. Surfaces should be wiped with a clean, li nt free cloth and all 
openings should be capped as quickly as possible. 
 
 Maintain fluid cleanliness 
 
 Maintain fluid temperature and viscosity to specifications 
 
 Maintain hydraulic system settings to specifications 
 
 Schedule component change outs before they fail 
 
 
 
NOTE 
 
IT IS THE OPERATOR’S RESPONSIBILITY TO MAINTAIN A CLEAN 
HYDRAULIC SYSTEM.

3.2 GENERAL DESCRIPTION 
 
Weatherford’s Mobile Gripper (MG) is a compact, truck -mounted rod injector with 
integral hydraulic power source and  minimum 4.5 metric tonne  knuckle picker 
with minimum 12 m eter reach. The MG is compatible  with any service rig or 
Flushby unit or other oilfield service equipment. 
 
The mobile injector unit  is structurally capable of pulling a 30,000lb static load. 
The unit is also capable of tripping a COROD string up to 1 5,000lbs if properly 
equipped. The MG’s main hydraulic pump is dedicated to driving the motors of 
the pulling unit. A second auxiliary pum p is used to power the safety system, 
chain tension, outriggers and picker controls. A third gear pump is used to force 
fluid back to the tank through the heat exchanger unit. 
 
The MG is specifically designed for rapid deployment and safe operation. In 
typical operating conditions, it rigs in or out in 30 minutes or less. A quick connect 
guide system, which may be assembled or disassembled in as little as 5 minutes, 
includes a swivel for reel or trailer placement  and a guide mouth safety system to 
safely control the COROD as it exits the guide. 
 
Due to its nature, the Mobile Gripper is not capable of many of the operations 
which are carried out in the normal service of the well. This means t hat the 
operations prior to and after removing or injecting the rod are typically attended 
to by the Service Rig/Flushby and its operators. The Mobile Gripper Operator’s 
job is to get rigged up, remove or inject the rod as quickly as possible, and get 
rigged out again. The service rig crew is required to assist with the rigging up and 
out as the timely accomplishment of these tasks also aids them in completing 
their tasks quickly and safely. 
 
It is in the best interest of the Mobile Gripper operator to develop a good working 
relationship with the crew members of the rigs they are working with. In order to 
perform the rigging up and riggin g out procedures efficiently, quickly and safely, 
assistance from the rig’s crew will be required.  A good working relationship will 
help to facilitate a cooperative and safe environment. 
 
The typical location of many of the Mobile Gripper’s components is shown on the 
following page. Keep in mind that this is not necessarily the position of these 
components on all Mobile Gripper units.  Parts of the Universal Mobile Gripper 
deck are shown in the Mobile Gripper Universal Deck Supplement.

3.2.1 The BOP Stack 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The BOP Stack is composed of several safety and service related elements. The 
Rod Safety Clamp is a hydraulically operated clamping device whose purpose is 
to capture the COROD in the event that the Injector loses its grip. The Safety 
operates on an externally checked, accumulator assisted circuit powered by the 
MG’s auxiliary pump. Its hydraulic circuit is constructed in such a way as to allow 
it to maintain pressure  for a short period of time  even when system pressure is 
lost.  
 
The Rod Wiper  contains a two piece rubber element which closes against the 
COROD to clean it of oil and wax as it is removed from the well. The rubber 
wipers inside the unit should be changed as necessary to effectively clean the 
passing COROD, and can be changed when the well is active as long as the 
Regan BOP is closed. To replace the rubbers, remove the Rod Wiper’s end 
caps. The rubbers can be removed from the open ends of the wiper body. Insert 
new rubbers and reinstall the end caps (shown on the next page).

The Regan BOP is another hydraulically actuated safety device designed to 
contain the pressure released when the well kicks. An auxiliary line connects to a 
quick coupler on the lower flange and allows the internal rubber bag to be 
inflated, closing around  the COROD and sealing the well. The BOP is not 
actuated by the MG’s pump driven hydraulic system, rather it is operated  either 
manually with a hand pump or using an independent air over hydraulic pump 
system to ensure that it can be closed under any circu mstance, regardless of the 
condition of the MG’s hydraulic system. 
 
The Flow Tee is simply an adapter with a port for a 1” quarter turn ball valve. This 
ball valve allows the well to be flushed or killed while the BOP stack is installed.  
 
The Hammer Union is the hardware which connects the BOP Stack to the well.  A 
Figure 400, 3” union rated to a minimum of 3000psi is required.

3.2.2 The Injector 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The Mobile Injector has two moveable pressure beams. The Pressure Beam 
cylinders force the Pressure Beams against the COROD and are infinitely 
adjustable up to the maximum system pressure. The Gripper Motors turn the 
gripper chains either clockwise or coun terclockwise to either inject or retrieve 
COROD from a well.

3.3 INJECTOR PERFORMANCE 
The Mobile Gripper Injector unit is a totally portable, fully adjustable hydraulic 
drive unit capable of injecting or retrieving COROD up to a dynamic string weight 
of 13 ,000lbs. The maximum static load that the Injector is rated for is 30,000lbs.  
The maximum load that the Injector is capable of lifting is directly related to the 
hydraulic pressure delivered to the Gripper Motors. It is important when surfacing 
a string that no mo re pressure is used than necessary to lift the weight of the 
COROD still in the well. Using the minimum required pressure ensures that any 
obstacle encountered on the way up  or down  the well will stop the Gripper 
Motors from turning, thus alerting the oper ator and preventing damage to  the 
COROD and the well tubing.  
 
NOTE: If the Injector is equipped with upgraded hydraulics, Gripper Pad 
housings and 3 part lifting apparatus a dynamic string weight of 15,000lbs 
can be lifted safely. However, without these u pgrades 13,000lbs is the 
maximum string weight which can be moved in the Injector.

3.4 UNDERSTANDING THE RO D SAFETY CLAMP AND 
INJECTOR PRESSURE BEAM CIRCUITS 
 
The Pressure Beam and Rod Safety Clamp are systems employed by all 
COROD mobile equipment  which inject COROD. Each has important features 
and uses which must be operated properly to ensure their effectiveness. 
 
Incorrect use of either of these systems can contribute to damage to COROD 
equipment, COROD Continuous Rod and injury to workers.  
 
Refer to the Universal Mobile Gripper Deck Supplement to see the specific 
differences in the placement of the accumula tors in the Pressure Beam and 
Safety Clamp circuits. 
3.4.1 The Pressure Beam Circuit 
 
The Pressure Beams use hydraulic power to squeeze the COROD between 
them. The pressure with which the rod is squeezed , the “Squeeze Pressure”, is 
controlled by the operator and  must be manipulated for various changing 
conditions. 
 
The Pressure Beam circuit is powered hydraulically by the System Pump. This is 
a pressure compensated pump designed to provide flow and pressure on 
demand. As soon as a function is activated, flow and pressure are delivered 
immediately.  
 
System pressure to the Pressure Beams is controlled by a non-adjustable 
Pressure Relief valve which limits squeeze pressure to no more than 2500psi. 
On Mobile Grippers this valve controls pressure for the Rod Safety Cl amp, 
Outriggers, Pressure Beam and Chain Tension circuits. This valve is mounted in 
the base of the picker and should never be adjusted by field staff. Only the 
Knuckle Picker sees unregulated pressure from the system pump.  
 
A check valve is located inlin e after the pump and prevents pressure from 
draining back while the pressure beams are closed for extended periods of time.  
 
From the check valve pressure is routed to the operator’s directional control 
valve through a manually adjustable Pressure Reducin g/Relieving valve. This 
allows the operator to modulate the squeeze pressure applied by the Pressure 
Beams. 
 
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18 

===== PAGE 31 =====
 
 
COROD® 
Continuous R od & Flushbys 
31 
© 2015 Weatherford International, Inc. 
This document is copy righted and, whether patentable or non -patentable subject matter, embodies the proprietary  and conf idential

shall be returned to Weatherf ord upon demand.  
 
A Nitrogen charged, 2.5 Gallon accumulator which is pre-charged to 900psi acts 
as a cushion to absorb any large anomalies which may pass through t he Injector 
such as welds or changes in rod size.  
 
The accumulator cannot be relied upon to maintain pressure in the Pressure 
Beam if the hydraulic pump ceases to function. In an emergency which causes 
the truck engine or hydraulic system to shut down i nternal leakage of the 
components in the system will allow pressure to bleed from the pressure beam 
circuit within a few minutes.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Accumulators and other hydraulic components should  be visually 
inspected for obvious deficiencies daily as part of the Level I and II 
Inspections. The Pressure Beam system is thoroughly inspected and 
tested at every annual inspection.

3.4.2 The Rod Safety Clamp Circuit 
 
The Rod Safety Clamp is a hydraulic device designed specifically to secure the 
COROD string when activated at the control panel in an emergency situation. It is 
intended to be use d only for a long enough period of time to install a mechanical 
Rod Clamp. Unlike the Pressure Beam the Safety’s sole function is to prevent the 
rod string from falling and is only to be used in an emergency.  
 
To be most effective the Rod Safety Clamp must be applied as quickly as 
possible by the operator in an emergency situation. If the rod begins to fall and is 
allowed to gain momentum it will be much more difficult for the safety to stop the 
rod.  
 
The circuit is powered by the System Pump and a check valve is installed  after 
the pump to prevent pressure from draining back. It uses an accumulator and 
system pressure relief valve as well.  
 
The Safety circuit is constructed with the speed of activation as a high priority. To 
achieve a very fast activation time large bore hoses are used and its 
accumulator, which is pre -charged to 1200psi, is teed into the extending side of 
the Safety Cylinders in  very close proximity to the cylinders themselves. This 
gives the Safety Accumulator’s pressure a very short and low resistance path to 
the Safety Cylinder. 
 
To further facilitate fast engagement, a remote directional valve  is used to 
activate the Safety. Rather than having the Safety Cylinders actuated directly by 
the operator’s control valve, when the contr ol valve is moved to the “On” or  “Off” 
position a remote, pilot operated shuttle is moved which opens and closes the 
Safety. This allows a more direct path to the Safety Cylinder.  
 
 
 
 
WARNING: 
THE ROD SAFETY CLAMP IS DESIGNED AS A BACK UP SAFETY 
SYSTEM AND SHOULD ONLY BE USED IN EMERGENCY 
SITUATIONS. IT IS NOT INTENDED, AND CANNOT BE RELIED 
UPON, TO SUSPEND A COROD STRING INDEFINITELY. ONCE 
THE SAFETY HAS BEEN APPLIED, INSTALL THE APPROPRIATE 
MECHANICAL ROD CLAMP IMMEDIATELY. 
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18

The Safety circuit must also be tested every day. To test the Rod Safety Clamp 
Accumulator: 
 
 Start the unit and engage the hydraulic system at the working engine 
speed specified for the unit. 
 Allow time for the accumulator to pressurize. This should only take a 
few moments. 
 Observe the safety pressure. The Safety Pressure gauge should read 
2500psi. If it does not investigate the cause. 
 Apply the Safety. 
 Disengage the hydraulic pump. 
 The safety should not experience a pressure drop of more than 10% 
(250psi) over a 10 minute period.  
 If the Safety Pressure drops more than 10%  in under 10 minutes, 
check the pre-charge on the accumulator and retest. 
 If the Safety Pressure continues to drop below acceptable limits the 
unit must be removed from service until the cause can be discovered 
and repaired. 
 
 
 
 
 
This Safety Accumulator test is to be performed  daily. In addition, the 
entire Rod Safety Clamp system is thoroughly inspected and tested every 
750 operating hours as part of the 750 hour  Preventive Maintenance 
Inspection.

SYSTEM AND SHOULD ONLY BE USED  IN EMERGENCY 
SITUATIONS. IT IS NOT INTENDED, AND CANNOT BE RELIED 
UPON, TO SUSPEND A COROD STRING INDEFINITELY. ONCE 
THE SAFETY HAS BEEN APPLIED, INSTALL THE APPROPRIATE 
MECHANICAL ROD CLAMP IMMEDIATELY.

3.5 THE KNUCKLE PICKER 
Each Mobile Gripper is equipped with an articulating knuckle picker crane. The 
crane is controlled by a set of joystick type directional control valves l ocated 
above the main operator’s control panel. These valves allow the crane to 
articulate around its base 360 º and to a distance of approximately 14 meters. 
There are a few different crane models in service on Mobile Grippers, each with 
its own specific l oad rating depending on the size and design of the crane. 
Consult the load rating chart affixed to your unit. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
For additional infor mation regarding differences in this equipment refer to 
the Universal Mobile Gripper Deck Supplement.

3.6 CONTROLS 
For additional infor mation regarding differences in the various controls 
refer to the Universal Mobile Gripper Deck Supplement. 
3.6.1 Outrigger Directional Control Valves 
The purpose of the outriggers is to provide a more stable base for the safer 
operation of the Mob ile Gripper. The picker should never be used without first 
extending the outriggers fully and leveling the Mobile Gripper unit.  
 
The quarter-turn ball valve behind and above the directional control valve bank 
shuts off hydraulic pressure to the picker con trols. The valve is shown in the 
closed position for operation of the outriggers or holding a load on the picker.

3.6.2 Knuckle Picker Controls 
The Mobile Gripper is equipped with an articulating knuckle picker with a lifting 
capacity of at least 4.5 metric tonnes. It is important to note that the lifting 
capacity of any articulating crane decreases significantly as the crane’s boom is 
extended.  
1. Telescope Out/In 
 Down to extend or up to 
retract the crane’s telescoping sections 
2. Top knuckle Up/Down 
 Down to raise or up to lower 
the top knuckle 
3. Lower knuckle Up/Down 
 Down to raise or up to lower 
the lower knuckle 
4. Rotate base 
 Down to rotate left or up to 
rotate right 
5. Picker Crane Lockout valve  (shown 
closed) 
 closed when not operating the 
crane or holding a load. Open to operate 
crane controls 
 
The Picker Crane has a load rating which 
decreases depending upon the length of the 
boom arm.  The load chart indicates that at 
14’1” the maximum load is 4993lbs. This is 
significant because the Injector unit weighs 
approximately 4200lbs. and should not be 
lifted a distance of more than 14 feet . Consult 
the load chart before lifting any heavy load. 
Some Mobile Gripper units are equipped with 
the Fassi F150A knuckle picker which has a 
slightly higher load rating. Make sure you 
consult the load chart to be certain  of the load 
rating of the unit you are using.

3.6.3 Optical Rod Counter (Optional) 
The Rod Counter consists of a sprocket driven optical encoder measuring 
bidirectional chain movement. This allows the counter to measure the length of 
the string when either retrieving or injecting rod.  A portable LCD display unit 
displays the depth location, the rate at which the rod is being transferrred and the 
temperature of the hydraulic fluid in the hydraulic reservoir. Data can be 
displayed in metric or imperial units. 
 
Remove the protective cover from the connector on the display unit. Ensure that 
both the male and female connectors are  clean and free of dirt or other debris 
before connecting the cable. Clean the contacts of the data cable and the  
receptacle before connecting the cable. Always replace the protective cap and 
store the display unit in the protective case in the truck cab when not in use. Do 
not start the truck engine when power to the counter is on as power surges may 
damage the unit . Place the display unit in a location which will be clearly visible 
while operating the gripper controls. 
 
The information cable screws into the 
side of the display unit.  Connect the 
cable then turn on the power . The 
power switch is located  either at the  
control panel or on the dash of the 
truck depending on the rig. 
 
Zero the counter by pressing and 
holding buttons 1 and 4 at the same 
time for 10 seconds.  Button 3 toggles 
the display from rate and depth 
reading to showing hydraulic 
temperature on the top line only.  
Button 2 toggles the units from metric 
to imperial. 
 
If a surge in power has affected the 
display unit’s memory you will see the 
screen go blank with the cursor 
flashing in the upper left corner. If this 
happens, the counter will no longer 
count and must be returned to the 
Edmonton Maintenance shop to be 
reconfigured.

3.6.4 Main Control Panel Upper Section 
 
 
 
The upper section of the main control panel contains the gauges which relate the 
condition of the various circuits of the unit as well as the emergency shutdown 
switch. 
 
1. Charge Pressure 
 The main pump which powers the gripper motors  contains a 
smaller charge pump within itself. This charge pump re -circulates 
some of the main pump’s case drain losses back to the main pump 
to effectively supercharge the main pump. The  charge pressure 
should always read between 300 – 400psi when in neutral and 250 
and 300psi during operation. Charge pressure should never read 
below 250psi.  If the charge pressure dr ops below 250psi the 
gripper motors may freewheel allowing the rod string to fall out of 
control.

ENGAGED AND THE APPROPRIATE COROD CLAMP INSTALLED 
UNTIL THE CAUSE OF THE PRESSURE LOSS CAN BE DETERMINED.

© 2015 Weatherford International, Inc.

2. System Pressure 
 The Auxiliary pump delivers pressure to all of the function s of the 
Mobile Gripper except the injector unit motors. The pressure in this 
circuit is regulated by a pressure relieving valve and should not 
exceed 2500psi. 
 
3. Picker Pressure 
 The picker requires the full output of the auxiliary pump to lift to it’s 
maximum capacity. Picker pressure may reach up to 4200psi. 
 
4. Chain Tension 
 The pressure in the chain tensioning circuit is increased by the 
operator as the chains wear and become more slack. Chain tension 
pressure should be as light as possible to tighten the chains , 
typically between 100 and 200psi. 
 
5. Squeeze Pressure 
 This is the pressure in the cylinders which move the moveable 
pressure beam against the COROD in the injector unit. This gauge 
may show up to 2500psi. 
 
6. Safety Pressure 
 The Safety provides a backup system for capturing the COROD in 
the event that the inje ctor unit loses pressure from the main pump 
or the COROD begins to fall for some reason. The safety pressure 
gauge should always show 4200psi when  the Mobile Gripper 
hydraulic system is active.  (At the time of this printing, the safety 
system is being changed to operate at system pressure of 2500psi. 
Some units have already been changed to meet this standard.) 
 
7. Down Pressure 
 This gauge shows the pressure being delivered to the gripper 
motors in the down direction of rotation. The closed loop pump 
which drives the gripper motors is set in the shop to deliver 5000psi 
and that is the maximum reading which should be seen on this 
gauge. 
 
8. Up Pressure 
 This gauge shows the pressure being delivered to the gripper 
motors in the up direction of rotation. It is similar to the down 
pressure reading in nature.

3.6.5 Main Control Panel Lower Section 
 
 
The lower portion of the main control panel houses the switches and adjustment 
knobs by which the operator controls the various functions of the Mobile Gripper 
unit. 
1. Chain Tension On/Off 
 Activates the chain tensioning cylinders to adjust the gripper chains 
2. Squeeze Pressure On/Off 
 Activates the cylinders in the moveable beam in the gripper unit  to 
apply or hold a grip on the COROD 
3. Gripper Brake On/Off 
 Activates the gripper brake circuit 
4. Safety On/Off 
 Activates the Safety clamp cylinder 
5. Safety Bleed Valve 
 After safety is activated and hydraulic system is disengaged , 
pressure can be relieved from the safety by opening this valve 
 Typically must be opened to relieve safety pressure before 
performing any hydraulic system maintenance or repairs 
 Will be left open while warming to allow fluid to circulate

6. Chain Tension Pressure Adjuster 
 Allows the 2500 psi relieved pressure supplied by the auxiliary 
pump to be reduced to a constant, reduced pressure  anywhere 
from 0 – 2500psi. Chain ten sion pressure should be used carefully 
as it is possible to damage the gripper chains if too much pressure 
is applied to the tensioning cylinders, typically 100 – 200psi. 
7. Squeeze Pressure Adjuster 
 Similar to the Chain Tension adjuster, this allows the 2500psi of the 
auxiliary pump to be reduced to any value from 0 – 2500psi. 
8. Up Pressure Adjustment 
 Allows the pressure delivered to the gripper motors, in the up  
direction of rotation, to be adjusted from charge pressure up to the 
maximum pressure set in the main pump. 
9. Down Pressure Adjustment 
 Allows the pressure delivered to the gripper motors , in the down 
direction of rotation, to be adjusted from charge pressure up to the 
maximum pressure set in the main pump. 
10. Gripper Motor Directional Joystick 
 This valve changes the direction of rotation and the volumetric flow 
of the main pump. This allows an infinite adjustability of the speed 
of the gripper motors.

3.6.6 Automatic BOP Pump 
Mobile Grippers are being fitted with an air over hydraulic automatic BOP 
inflation system like the one shown below. This consists of a small hydraulic 
pump driven by a pneumatic motor. The pneumatic motor is powered by the truck 
chassis air system and the hydraulic pump has its own dedicated reservoir of 
fluid from which to fill the BOP.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Check the fluid level in the reservoir at the sight glass on the left side of the case. 
Ensure that the truck air pressure is at 120psi and the bleed valve is closed. 
Connect the fire proof hose to the BOP and move the switch to the ON position. 
Use the regulator to bring the BOP pressure up to 1000psi. Once up to pressure, 
the pump should hold that pressure. If the pump does not maintain the pressure 
in the BOP the hand pump can still be used to increase pressure in the BOP. The 
Automatic BOP pump is also equipped with a second hand pump (not yet 
installed in this picture)  in addition to the hand pump mounted on the bumper in 
case of an air system or pump failure. 
 
Do not use the automatic BOP pump system if it does not hold pressure in the 
BOP.

3.6.7 Oil Cooler Bypass Switch 
The hydraulic system circulates its fluid through a radiator style cooling system 
with thermostatically regulated fan. When this switch is in the “auto” position, the 
fan is regulated by the temperature of the fluid via a temperature s ensing 
thermocouple in the cooler radiator. This is the preferred setting for winter 
operation. When switched to the “Manual” position, the fan will run constantly  
when the PTO is engaged in hydraulics mode . On warm summer days, this will 
keep the hydraulic oil at its coolest temperature. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
For additional information regarding control  of the cooling fans refer to the 
Universal Mobile Gripper Deck Supplement.

3.6.8  Engine/Hydraulic Tank Heater 
 
The Mobile Gripper is equipped with a circulation type engine coolant heater. In 
cold weather conditions, the engine coolant is circulated through the heater 
where diesel fuel is burned to heat the coolant and warm the engine. The engine 
heater re duces idle time  decreasing engine wear and increasing  fuel savings. 
Once the engine is warm, the circuit can be diverted so that the engine  circulates  
coolant through the hydraulic tank to warm the hydraulic fluid. The heating unit 
does not have to be tur ned on when the engine is used to heat the hydraulic tank 
as the engine’s water pump will circulate the coolant. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The engine heater control unit is 
mounted in the cab of the truck. The 
system is activated by pressing the 
“Manual” button, or It can be set to 
come on automatically at a specified 
time using the “Timer Set” button.

The engine heater and 
its associated circuits are designed for seasonal use.  The various valves located 
on the Mobile Gripper allow the operator to manage the temperature of the 
hydraulic reservoir, toolbox and engine coolant and must be monitored to 
function correctly. Leaving the tank heater circuit open in warm weather is very 
detrimental to the condition of the hydraulic fluid. Similarly, failure to properly 
warm the hydraulic fluid in cold weather can cause unpredictable effects in the 
way some of the hydraulic functions operate and damage to components can 
occur. Typically the pumps are damaged first. Starting the truck engine in very 
cold weather without properly warming the coolant causes accelerated engine 
wear and will cause the truck to require excessive idle time.  
 
The purpose of the engine heater and associated hydraul ic warming circuit is to 
maintain a safe operating temperature in the  engine coolant and hydraulic fluid at 
cold temperatures . The hydraulic fluid must be kept between 0ºC and 50ºC 
through careful monitoring of fluid temperature and management of the heater 
circuit. It is neither sufficient nor acceptable for operators  to turn the heater on 
and walk away.  The engine/hydraulic reservoir heater must be managed by  the 
operator.

3.6.10 Transfer Case Engagement and Manual Bypass 
 
This electric toggle switch activates a pneumatic valve. This valve actuates a 
pneumatic cylinder which switches  the transfer case from “Travel” to 
“Hydraulics”.  
 
For additional infor mation regarding the PTO engagement controls refer to 
the Universal Mobile Gripper Deck Supplement. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The valve which controls the transfer case actuator is located behind the driver’s 
seat and can be activated manually by turning the red dial 180º. This allows the 
transfer case to be engaged in the event that the electrical switch is not operable.

3.7 COROD CLASSIFICATION AND DIMENSIONS 
 
The COROD continuous sucker rod is available in a range of grades and sizes.  
 
There are five grades of COROD; 
 D Carbon Steel 
 DE “Endura” Alloy Steel 
 SE Special “Endura” Alloy Steel 
 DW Alloy Steel 
 SW Special Alloy Steel 
 
The D , DE and DW  grades of COROD differ only in their resistance to certain 
chemicals which may be present in the well. DE COROD is alloyed with 
Chromium and Molybdenum which may help to  resist CO2 assisted corrosion 
better than D grade COROD. DW COROD is alloyed with Chromium, Nickel and 
Molybdenum and may exhibit corrosion resistance in different environments. D, 
DE and DW products are suitable for medium load applications.  
 
The Special Alloy SE grade has 
identical chemical composition to 
DE grade COROD, but with higher 
tensile and yield strength . 
Similarly, SW has identical 
chemical composition to DW, but 
with higher tensile and yield 
strength. Both are  suitable for use 
in deep, high production wells.  
 
 
Specifications and dimensions for 
the standard strength Endura 
COROD are shown in Figure 31.

however progressive cavity pumps  are driven exclusively by round COROD 
strings.

Plunger size is only applicable with wells using reciprocating rod pumps (RRP) 
and will be left as “000” for wells using progressing cavity pumps (PCP). A bulk 
string sent from a plant is assigned string tags in alpha sequence. 
When an installation is performed and a section of COROD is taken from the reel 
a bulk string, the operator stamps the tag with a letter in the string number box. 
The letter “A” is used for the first string taken off the reel, “B” for the second and 
so on until the reel is empty. The tag is then attached to the well in which the 
string is installed.  The string tag is installed in a location which will be certain to 
remain with the well. If the COROD string is removed from the well, the string tag 
must be fastened to it so that it can be identified.  
 
When a tapered COROD string is modified an “M” is used at the end of the string 
code, e.g. SE-746-150-68-LV-12058M.

3.9 MOBILE GRIPPER™ EQUIPMENT INSPECTIONS 
Frequent inspections increase the likelihood of detecting real and potential 
problems. The sooner problems are identified and repaired the less likely they 
are to generate failures. Each level of inspection is performed at different times 
under different circumstances and in some cases by different personnel. It is the 
commitment of Weatherford to make every effort reasonable to prevent failures 
of critical systems from occurring. Refer  to the  “Corig™ Level III Overhead 
Lifting Equipment Inspection ” Form 1-1-GL-GL-CSR-00004 manual for more 
detailed information and instruction on performing inspections. 
 
When MG™ crews are audited it will be necessary to produce evidence that 
major inspectio ns have been performed. Ensure that serial numbers of major 
inspection forms are recorded in the operations logbook if the original copy is not 
retained. 
 
3.9.1 Deck & Chassis Inspections 
Including but not limited to: 
- Outrigger assemblies 
- Rear bumper 
- Headache racks 
- Knuckle Picker 
- Hydraulics 
- Rod Guide 
 
Level I Daily Inspection 
It is the responsibility of all operators to be aware of the condition of all parts of 
the rig. On a daily basis, operators should do a “walk around” of the rig. The 
operator(s) should be watching for obvious deficiencies in the condition of the 
mast, hydraulic controls and actuators, guy lines etc. 
 
Level III Annual Inspection 
On a yearly basis, industry regulations require a complete inspection be 
conducted by qualified personnel. Weatherford will perform this inspection every 
750 operating hours or annually, whichever comes first.  
 
Level IV Recertification 
Every 24,000 operating hours (typically about 10 years of service) the MG™ 
must be  inspected by a professional engineer. In this case the structure is  
sandblasted and all welds are tested using a non -destructive test (NDT) 
inspection process. Because the time between recertification may be very long 
each of the other test levels are vital to ensure the safe condition of the MG™. 
This extended duration  is only permissible if Level I  and III inspections are

Monthly Inspection

operations while on  a job. The intent of this inspection is to ensure that each of 
the common systems is in proper functioning order on a monthly basis and that 
crew members are performing certain important day to day tasks appropriately.  
3.9.2 Overhead Lifting Equipment Inspections 
Including but not limited to: 
- Rod Elevators   - Rod Hooks    
- Self-locking hooks  - Lift Mandrel 
- Slings, nylon and wire 
 
Level I  
Visual observation of the equipment by the crew prior to and/or during  operation 
and/or during routine service. 
 
Level II  
Level I inspection plus a more thorough inspection of load areas by the operator  
to check for proper lubrication, obvious external cracks,  premature wear or 
deterioration, missing parts or guards. 
 
Level III  
Level III inspection requires that the equipment be thoroughly checked in the field 
to determine the serviceability. This may, at the inspector’s discretion,  include 
non destructive testing (NDT) techniques on load areas and may  require some 
minor disassembly of guards. This inspection must be document ed in the the 
Weatherford Mobile Gripper ™ Operations Logbook . Technical assistance  
provided for the inspection could be an NDT technician, a senior well servicing  
individual designated by the company, or engineer. This person would be  
required to supervis e or assist in the inspection and sign off in the Weatherford 
Mobile Gripper ™ Operations Logbook  that the inspection was completed. Any  
major repairs will require that a Level IV inspection be completed on the piece of  
equipment in question.

Level IV Recertification 
A Level IV inspection would require the equipment to be disassembled and must  
be inspected by a professional engineer or an original equipment manufacturer 
(OEM) or his agent. This inspection should, at the inspector’s discretion, includ e 
NDT of all critical load bearing areas. All deficiencies  and repairs wi ll be 
corrected. 
3.9.3 BOP Inspections 
Including: 
- BOP Stack 
 
Level I  
Visual observation of the equipment by the crew during  daily operations and/or 
function test for mechanical defects and proper operating condition. 
 
Level III  
Level III inspection requires that during installation of the BOP on a well or prior 
to any pressure test, a thorough visual inspection of the assembly and parts is to 
be conducted for structural or operating defect s.  This inspection must be 
documented in the Weatherford Mobile Gripper ™ Operations Logbook . The 
person performing the inspection would normally be the operator, who must 
possess a Well Servicing BOP certificate. Other persons qualified would be a 
senior well servicing  individual, designated by the company, or professional 
engineer. This person would be  required to supervise or assist in the inspection 
and sign off in the Weatherford Mobile Gripper ™ Operations Logbook  that the 
inspection was completed.  
 
Level IV Recertification 
A Level IV inspection is conducted every three years. The equipment is 
disassembled and thoroughly inspected for structural or operating defects, the 
measurement of critical dimensions and  non destructive testing (NDT)  of all 
critical areas. This requires inspection by a professional engineer or an original 
equipment manufacturer (OEM) or their agent. All deficiencies  will be corrected. 
Certification documents for the BOP equipment must be present and easily 
accessible.

4 SAFETY 
4.1 WEATHERFORD GLOBAL SAFETY MANUAL 
4.1.1 Health, Safety, Security and Environmental Policy Statement 
Weatherford International, Inc. (“the Company”) is committed to pursuing the 
highest standards of health, safety, security and environmental performance. It is 
the po licy of the Company to be in compliance with all applicable laws  and 
regulations within the areas of our operation, to conduct all operations in a 
manner that promotes safe work practices and avoids risk to our employees, our 
neighbors, and the environment , and to implement the programs,  training, and 
internal controls necessary to achieve these goals. 
 
It is Management’s goal that no one suffers an injury or illness and that no harm 
comes to the environment. We are  committed to protecting the integrity of the 
Company’s human, physical, and financial resources. The Company’s  objectives 
are: 
 To conduct our operations with minimal risk to personnel or the 
environment. 
 To seek continuous improvement. 
 To consult with all of our employees, as well as with others,  with regards 
to quality, health, safety, security and the environment. 
 In order to achieve these objectives, the Company shall: 
 Consult, listen and respond openly to our clients, employees, neighbors, 
contractors and others who may work with us. 
 Work with partners, suppliers, competitors, industry groups, and regulators 
to raise HSSE standards within our industry. 
 Report our performance openly. 
 Recognize those within the Company who contribute to improving HSSE 
performance. 
 At a minimum, comply fully with all legislative requirements, and 
implement stricter standards when those  legislative requirements are 
insufficient. 
 Provide suitable and sufficient training for our staff and others such that 
we have a technically competent staff and an active HSSE culture. 
 Make it known that NO ACTIVITY IS SO IMPORTANT THAT WE 
CANNOT TAKE THE TIME TO DO IT SAFELY. 
 All employees not only have the right, but the obligation to stop work if 
conditions are unsafe. 
 Participate in risk management, loss control, and monitoring pr ocesses, 
both internally and with our clients,  to provide the assurance that our 
HSSE Management Systems are functioning correctly and that hazards  
and risks are assessed and minimized.

4.2 MANAGEMENT OF CHANGE 
The Weatherford Global Safety Manual  provides a way to make  permanent 
changes in process,  procedure, organization, personnel or other operating 
factors can be evaluated for their impact on HSSE or other business aspects and  
approved before implementation. 
 
The Management of Change process (MOC) serves to: 
 prevent unnecessary or counterproductive changes; 
 ensure changes do not adversely affect HSSE, operations or the level of 
service to the customer; 
 ensure no changes are made by individuals without knowledge and/or  
agreement of all relevant parties; 
 ensures interfaces with all relevant parties are taken into account before  
committing to the change; and 
 produce a record of the change rationale and change assessment process 
 
The MOC process needs to be implemented if there is a change to: 
 Operations/facility specific procedures. 
 Regulations affecting 
operations/facilities. 
 Operating criteria. 
 Key personnel. 
 Company organization. 
 Safety systems or critical operational 
systems. 
 Equipment operating software. 
 
For more detailed information on the 
Management of Change  process or Variance 
Procedures contact Weatherford COROD 
Global Business Unit management.

4.3 WEATHERFORD COROD MG OPERATIONS LOGBOOK 
 
Each COROD Mobile Gripper operator is provided a Weatherford MG Operations 
Logbook. The purpose of the logbook is to track every maintenance or inspection 
event that occurs with the unit from the daily inspections all the way up to any 
major repairs that are performed.  
 
Each book is set up to record twelve months of daily, weekly and monthly 
information. The first month listed for entering information is May as the book is 
set up to begin  and end with each fiscal year. Once it is completely filled out, it is 
required to stay with the rig. 
 
Part of the operating history of the rig is the daily sheet which must be filled out in 
full and kept updated. It consists of:  
 
 Daily Inspection checklist 
 Weekly grease and inspection 
 Weekly BOP emergency response drills 
 Monthly rig inspection signoff 
 
There are also locations in the logbook for: 
 Component certification inspections 
 Preventive maintenance log 
 Deficiency and repair log 
 
Refer to the “ Daily and Weekly Grease and Inspection ” and the “ Mobile 
Gripper Monthly and Annual Inspection ” manuals for further information on 
performing inspections. Refer to Technical Bulletin 114  for further Operations 
Logbook information. 
 
It is imperative and mandatory that the Operations Logbook is kept up to date 
with accurate data. The completeness and accuracy of the logbook are subject 
to inspection both by Weatherford inter nal auditors and government controlling 
bodies.

4.4 PERSONAL PROTECTIVE EQUIPMENT 
 
All rig operating personnel must wear Approved Eye Protection, Flame Resistant 
Clothing, Head Protection and Foot Protection as outlined by Section 12 of the 
Weatherford Global Safety Manual  or the appropriate standard in your local 
jurisdiction. 
 
Weatherford is responsible to ensure 
that operators are trained in the correct 
use, care, limitations and assigned 
maintenance of the personal protective 
equipment.  
 
It is the operator’s responsibility to use 
and wear properly the appropriate 
personal protective equipment, inspect 
the personal protective equipment 
before using it and to not use personal 
protective equipment which is 
inadequate for the fun ction for which it 
was designed (i.e. worn or damaged). 
 
Gloves are strongly recommended and 
typically used by operators for 
protection from contact with chemicals 
and pinch points. 
 
It is Weatherford COROD policy to we ar only clean, oil free personal protective 
equipment for reasons of both safety and professionalism. 
 
4.5 FIRST AID 
 
According to the Weatherford Global Safety Manual Section 3,  a Personal First 
Aid Kit must be within close proximity to the job site. As a min imum, Weatherford 
requires that a No.2 First Aid kit be stored in the cab of the mobile gripper at all 
times. Consult the appropriate authority in your local jurisdiction for first aid 
requirements.

4.6 NOISE LEVELS AND HEARING PROTECTION 
 
The Weatherford Gl obal Safety Manual requires that all personnel have access 
to hearing protection when exposed to continuous sound levels higher than 
85dB. 
 
Sound levels have been measure at points around the MG as follows: 
 
 Control panel – 78dB 
 Mid-truck – 85dB 
 At the engine – 90dB 
 
When working near the front of the Mobile Gripper during periods of operation 
either ear plugs or sound cancelling ear muffs must be worn.

4.7 GAS DETECTION 
 
Section 18 in the Weatherford Global Safety Manual refers to the detection of 
dangerous gases. Sour Gas (H 2S) is a deadly gas which is commonly released 
from crude wells. Low levels of H 2S may not be deadly  however the presence of 
even small amounts of sou r gas must be detected and handled with extreme 
care. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
There are 2 commonly used types of personal gas 
detection devices. One is a disposable, single gas 
detector used to detect the presence of Hydrogen Sulfide 
gas. This unit has a 100 day expiration and is disposed 
of at the end of that 100 day period. Recali bration of this 
unit is not required unless the unit displays a self test 
failure. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
FIGURE 37: DISPOSABLE H2S MONITOR 
WARNING 
H2S GAS HAS AN ODOR SIMILAR TO ROTTEN EGGS. THE 
ODOR OF H 2S GAS CAN BE DETECTED AT LEVELS AS 
LOW AS 10PPB (PARTS PER BILLION). AT 50 – 100PPM 
(PARTS PER MILLION) THE HUMAN SENSE OF SMELL CAN 
FAIL AND EYE IRRITATION, DIZZINESS, COUGHING, AND 
HEADACHE CAN OCCUR. AT HIGH EXPOSURES (USUALLY 
GREATER THAN 300PPM), H 2S HAS THE AMAZING EFFECT 
OF CAUSING THE NOSE TO STOP PERCEIVING ITS SMELL 
AFTER A FEW INHALATIONS, WHICH MAY LEAD TO THE 
INHALATION O F A TOXIC OR FATAL DOSE (WHICH CAN 
OCCUR AT 600PPM).  
 
AT THE FIRST SIGN OF THE PRESENCE OF H 2S GAS THE 
AREA AROUND THE WELL MUST BE EVACUATED AS 
QUICKLY AS POSSIBLE.

4.8 PROPER LIFTING AND BACK SAFETY 
 
Section 2 of the Weatherford Global Safety Manual contains information 
regarding safe conduct in the workplace. Proper lifting technique is critical to 
back safety, but perhaps more important is proper planning. Before you lift that 
box, or tool, or piece of equipment, take a moment to consider your action: 
 
 Do you need to lift the item manually?  
 How heavy is it?  
 Where are you moving the item from?  
 Where does it have to go?  
 What route do you have to follow? 
 
If the item needs to be moved manually, and i t is heavy or ungainly, ask for help. 
When using mechanical help, remember to push, not pull - you'll have more 
control, and greater leverage. Fasten the load to the equipment, so sudden stops 
or vibration doesn’t jar it off. When moving an item from a hard-to-reach place be 
sure to position yourself as close to the load as possible.  Check your path from 
place to place and remove tripping hazards. Stabilize uneven or loose ground, or 
choose an alternate route. The shortest way isn't always the fastest, or t he 
safest. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Squat to lift; do not bend at the waist. Keep the load close to your body and 
stagger your feet if possible. If you must turn while carrying the load, do not twist, 
turn with your feet. 
 
 
 
Virginia Commonwealth University. (2003) Back Safety and Lifting Techniques  
http://www.vcu.edu/oehs/fire/safetytech.html 
FIGURE 41: HOW TO PROPERLY LIFT A LOAD

4.9 OVERHEAD POWER LINES 
 
Anytime the Mobile Gripper’s Knuckle Picker is used, whether it is on a lease or 
not, at least 7 meters of clearance from overhead power lines to any part of the 
picker or the load it is lifting  must exist. I fit is not possible to maintain this 
clearance the power source mu st be shut down before any lifting can be done. 
Refer to Section 24.4 of the Weatherford Global Safety Manual. 
4.10 HOT WORK PROCEDURE 
 
According to the Weatherford Global Safety Manual Section 39.12, Hot Work is 
defined as work in which a flame is used or sparks or other sources of ignition 
may be produced, including: 
 
 cutting, welding, burning, air gouging, riveting, drilling, grinding, and chipping. 
 using electrical equipment not classified for use in a hazardous environment. 
 introducing a combustion engine to a work process. 
  
Hot work in a hazardous environment may not begin until a Hot Work Permit is 
issued, the hot work location is suitably isolated from explosive materials and 
testing shows that the atmosphere does not contain more than the minimum 
allowable amount of flammable substances. Testing must be performed at 
regular intervals appropriate to the hazard associated with the work. 
 
This means that when a Field Welder is used, it must be located at least 25 
meters from any well and regular testing  must be done for the presence of 
combustible gases.

4.11 SITE HAZARD ASSESSMENT 
 
When arriving on site to perform a job, prior to any work beginning and before 
performing a Job Safety Analysis (JSA), the state of the work site needs to be 
assessed and recorded. 
 
The intent of the Site Hazard Assessment is to make everyone on site, as well as 
those arriving during your job, aware of any potential hazards that may exist with 
the work location itself. Although it is a checklist, specific information such as 
evacuation points still need to be recorded.  The site hazard assessment form can 
be found in Appendix L of this manual.

4.12 JOB SAFETY ANALYSIS (JSA) 
 
Once the site has been deemed safe for work, a Job Safety Analysis (JSA) is to 
be written for each job, task or procedure that is performed at a Weatherford 
facility or at a customer’s location. Employees can use a standard JSA is it exists 
regionally. 
 
The purpose of the J SA is to help bring awareness to the potential hazards on a 
job site and find ways to eliminate or lessen the likelihood of those hazards. 
There are two parts to a JSA 
 Basic Job Steps and Corrective Actions 
 Verification of Team Understanding 
4.12.1  Basic Job Steps and Corrective Actions 
Each job can be broken down to basic steps. The first section of the JSA is for 
breaking down a job into its basic steps and helps identify preventative or 
corrective actions. There is space to break down a job to a maximum of ten basic 
steps.

4.12.2 Job Review and Verification  
The second section of the JSA is to verify that all personnel that are involved in 
the job have discussed and reviewed the potential hazards. It is the final 
confirmation that all personnel understand the risks and to make certain that 
everyone on site is aware that anyone has the right to stop working or stop the 
job if they identify any unsafe conditions or practices.

4.13 RADAR 
While a Job Safety Analysis is a fantastic tool, it doesn’t always address the 
personal planning or the planning of continuous routine activates. With RADAR, 
the idea is to have employees always thinking about safety so that safety 
becomes a habit. 
 
4.13.1 Recognize the Hazard 
 Purpose: Observe the whole “picture”-hazards, environment, behaviors 
and actions. 
 
 Key considerations: 
1. Be open minded 
2. Observe people interaction with hazards and their environment 
3. Observe people behaviors 
4. Use your senses (sight, sound, touch, smell) 
5. Balance observations- see safe and unsafe 
4.13.2 Approach the Situation or Person 
 Purpose: Make safe the area and the activity  
 
 Key considerations:  
1. Get employee’s (or other key workers) attention: “I was 
concerned about your safety” or “I was watching you work and I 
was concerned about your safety” 
2. Ensure all work is shut down 
3. Make surrounding areas safe 
4.13.3 Discuss the Unsafe Condition or Act 
 Purpose: Gain a shared understanding of issues and what happened 
 
 Key considerations: 
1. Ask about the activity that the employee is doing. 
2. Ask questions to understand what the employee is thinking. 
3. Use open questions- what, where, how and when (or describe) 
– to gain understanding. 
4. Take time to listen and understand the issues of your fellow 
employee. Think about what pressure she/he is under 
5. Listen, then confirm what you’ve learned

4.14 COMMUNICATION ON THE JOB 
 
The importance of effective communication on the job site cannot be overstated. 
Accurately relaying your intensions and expectations to the workers around you 
is the single most important preventative measure toward creating a safe job site. 
It is very imp ortant that good communication practices be d iscussed in the pre -
job safety meeting and that these practices get carried out while performing the 
job. 
 
Typically, the lead hand of the Service Rig/Flushby  crew will take a leadership 
role when coordinating and delegating the completion of tasks on a given job. 
The Mobile Gripper operator should take cues from the lead hand and only when 
performing Mobile Gripper specific operations should the MG operator ta ke the 
lead. 
 
It is important that each member of the crew inform the others if there is any 
change in the conditions of the lease or equipment. During particularly hazardous 
tasks such as overhead lifts, it is important that the operator of the lifting de vice 
coordinates the activities of the other workers involved in the lift.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
4.14.1 Hand Signals 
Hand signals are generally used in guiding vehicles. Hand signals play a 
vital role in safety as they can help to prevent accidents and injuries that

4.15  ENERGY LOCKOUT 
4.15.1 Well Service Energy Isolation 
 
Power equipment like the surface drive system and production piping represent a 
significant hazard to personnel working on the well. Whenever work is performed 
on a well it must be verified that electrical pow er sources are disengaged and 
pressurized piping is closed and secured before work begins. The two primary 
types of equipment that require lockout are drive heads and pump jacks. 
 
When locking out an electrical box ensure that electrical power is 
turned off to the well being serviced. If the box is already locked, 
add your lock as a back up. Attach a lock out tag to the lock with 
the operator’s name, the Weatherford company name and  the 
operator’s phone number.  
 
When isolating the flow lines, a minimum of one block valve must 
be closed and locked out before work can be started. When possible, attempt to 
close and lock out at least two block valves. As with the electrical box, always  
attach a lock out tag.  
 
Refer to Section 29 of the Weatherford Global Safety Manual  for information 
regarding safety lockout procedures.

4.15.2 Field Maintenance Power Lockout 
 
There are times  that Weatherford Mobile Equipment will require maintenance.  In 
the event that this becomes necessary, it is imperative that all power systems 
(i.e. PTO) are isolated prior to beginning the required maintenance. 
 
When servicing the equipment, ensure the power is turned off 
before the service is performed. Remove the keys from the 
vehicle and place them in a lock box if available. Attach a lock 
out tag to the lock box and/or the steering wheel of the vehicle 
with the operator’s name, the Weatherford company name and 
the operator’s phone number.  
 
 
In the event that the mobile equipment is required to be engaged (performing 
system checks, troubleshooting, etc.) while performing service, a JSA MUST be 
performed prior to starting the equipment. The JSA is to address all hazards and 
emergency response plans prior to work commencing.  Once any issues are 
assessed, the unit must once again be locked out and the repairs or 
maintenance can continue.

4.16 PULLING/MOVING COROD EQUIPMENT ON LEASE 
 
Weatherford COROD has specific policies regarding towing a nd pulling its 
equipment. The Mobile Gripper unit is manufactured with specifically designed 
pulling points and should not be pulled or towed from any point on the frame or 
deck but the points designated for doing so.  
 
Weatherford equipment is not intended to be used as a towing vehicle and it is 
not permissible to use any piece of Weatherford mobile equipment to pull or tow 
another vehicle. If it is necessary to tow or pull any piece of COROD mobile 
equipment a designated tow vehicle must be used (i.e. a bulldozer or adequately 
sized tow truck  or tractor) and before the vehicle is moved  approval to move the 
vehicle must be given by the operator’s field supervisor and a Job Safety 
Observation must be performed and documen ted and must include the signature 
of each worker involved in the operation. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
It is the operator’s responsibility to ensure that the COROD equipment is used in 
a way which does not increase the risk that the equipment will be damaged. In 
the event that a customer requests that a piece of COROD equipment be moved 
in a way which the o perator feels may involve undue risk or if the condition of the 
lease or its approaches are in such a condition that traveling on th em represents 
a potential hazard to the equipment , the COROD Equipment Release Form must 
be completed and signed by a repres entative of the customer accepting 
responsibility for any damage that may occur to the unit as a result of a 
maneuver which deviates from the Fleet Safety Manual accepted practices. 
WARNING: 
 
NO WEATHERFORD EQUIPMENT MAY EVER BE TOWED OR PULLED SIDEWAYS. 
DAMAGE TO THE EQUIPMENT IS ALMOST CERTAIN TO OCCUR AND DANGER TO 
PERSONNEL IS VERY HIGH

4.16.1 Tire Chains 
 
When driving through adverse driving conditions such as snow and mud, it may 
be necessary to install tire chains. Tire chain s can help prevent Weatherford 
COROD products and services mobile equipment from getting stuck or sliding.  
 
Depending on the unit, chains on the rear drive wheels will either be single or 
triple style chains. Each unit will also be equipped with one steering chain to be 
installed on the front driver side tire. Despite these differences, the tire chains will 
all be installed in the same way. 
 
The outriggers can be used to assist in the installation however, When deploying 
the outriggers extreme caution  must be taken to ensure the wheel chocks are 
used at all times on at least one wheel that is touching the ground. Extra 
attention must be paid wh en lifting the rear of the unit . The front brakes are 
application brakes only and do not have an emergency brake function.  
 
 
1. Lay chains out flat, removing all tangles. When 
chains are placed on tire, cross chain hook eyes 
must face away from the tire. Damage to your 
tires could result if hooks face against tire. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
2. Lay chain over tire, cross chain hooks up and 
fastener on outside of tire before locking:

Tire Chain Notes: 
 
Tire chain is especially hardened for the application intended. This hardening 
makes the chain hard and brittle compared to lifting chains. Under high tension 
the chain can suddenly break with no warning and pieces of metal may fly. Life is 
endangered when tire chains are used for unintended purposes such as towing, 
tie-down and lifting. 
 
Tire chains are used during difficult driving conditions and tire chains may break. 
Driving with tire chains requires special consideration. Never drive faster than 50 
km/h (30mph).The driver must always observe the potential  risks and drive at a 
safe speed. 
 
Always use tire chains when difficult driving conditions are expected and before 
getting in trouble. The chains are not made for spinning the unit out of trouble, 
but to increase the tire grip and reduce operating risks. 
 
A chain must never be used when one or more links are worn more than half the 
thickness of the cross section. Never operate with defective chains. Chain 
damages must be repaired immediately. 
 
In case of knocking or torn chains stop the vehicle immediately , increase tension 
of knocking chains, dismount or repair torn chains. 
 
Be aware of local regulations governing the use of tire chains on bare asphalt. 
Tire chains on bare asphalt will reduce the road grip. Generally avoid using tire 
chains on bare asphalt whenever possible. 
 
It is always a special challenge to get a good grip on hard ice and rocks. Too 
much steel (chain) on the tire will make it even more difficult i.e. less is often 
better. Some heavy chains may actually reduce the grip. Show extreme caution 
 
Doubling the vehicle speed quadruples the stress on the chain. High speed, even 
in very short periods, may introduce invisible cracks in the chain surface. Such 
cracks will eventually develop into a full breakdown, even during normal careful 
operations. This may sometimes explain an “inexplicable” breakage. 
 
The stress on the chain is considerably reduced when the chain is properly 
tightened. A well tightened chain “rolls” easily. 
 
Tire chains will cause tire wear. A chain that’s fixed to the tire will  create grooves. 
The chain must always be allowed to move on the tire during operations. 
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18

4.17 ROD SAFETY CLAMP 
 
The Rod Safety Clamp is one of the critical safety features of the Mobile 
Gripper’s equipment. It is used in an emergency situation such as a loss o f 
system pressure or loss of grip  in the Injector. To engage the Rod Safety Clamp, 
the Safety Bleed Valve must be closed. The Safety On/Off valve activates the 
Rod Safety Clamp. Move the valve actuation lever DOWN to activate the safety.  
 
The Rod Safety Clamp is the Mobile Gripper  Injector’s primary safety backup and 
should only be applied in emergency situations. The Safety must be tested daily, 
however should only be tested on itself without  COROD. This will preserve the 
pads in the safet y so that they will be in the best possible condition when 
required in an emergency.  
 
If the Injector begins to lose grip on the COROD and control cannot be regained, 
apply the Rod Safety Clamp and immediately install the appropriate COROD 
Clamp. If the h ydraulic system has become disengaged for any reason, the 
pressure in the Safety Accumulator will not last indefinitely.  
 
In an emergency the Rod Safety Clamp should be activated before activating the 
Emergency Shut Down. The accumulator system is designe d to provide enough 
reserve pressure to close the Clamp without the Hydraulics engaged, but it is 
preferable to engage the Safety Clamp prior to activating the Emergency Shut 
Down. If it is safe to do so, a COROD Clamp should be installed on the COROD 
soon after the Rod Safety Clamp has been applied as the hydraulic pressure 
available with the hydraulics disengaged will begin to bleed down after several 
minutes.

4.18 ROD SAFETY CLAMP & EMERGENGY RESPONSE 
FOR PREVENTION OF LOSS OR POTENTIAL LOSS OF ROD STRING 
 
The rod safety clamp is one of the critical safety features of a Mobile Gripper. It is 
designed to be used in a situation such as a loss of system pressure or loss of 
grip in the mobile injector and is also a necessary preventive measure against 
accidental loss of control of the COROD string.  
 
The safety On/Off valve activates the rod  safety clamp. Move the valve actuation 
lever DOWN to activate. 
 
Emergency Response Procedure 
If a situation arises where the rod starts freefalling or has the potential* to do so: 
1. Activate the Safety Clamp. 
2. Signal the emergency to personnel (If possible use one long horn blast). 
3. Evacuate** to a safe distance as quickly as possible. 
4. When the rod has stopped moving locate all personnel and make certain 
they are unharmed. 
5. Immediately return to the well and apply two rod clamps. 
6. Contact area supervisor once control has been established. 
 
**Only evacuate the area if  the rod is freely moving. 
If it is stationary, secure the rod immediately. 
 
At times it may be necessary to hit the emergency shut 
down switch as well. Before activating the Emergency 
Shut Down, the Rod Safety Clamp must be activated 
first. The accumulato r system is designed to provide 
enough reserve pressure to close the Clamp without 
the Hydraulics engaged, but it is preferable to engage 
the Safety Clamp prior to activating the Emergency 
Shut Down. Apply rod clamps when safe to do so. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
WARNING 
ONCE SAFETY IS APPLIED 
AND THE COROD HAS 
BECOME STATIONARY A 
ROD CLAMP MUST BE 
INSTALLED QUICKLY. THE 
SAFETY ACCUMULATOR 
WIILL ONLY MAINTAIN 
HYDRAULIC PRESSURE FOR 
A LIMITED LENGTH OF TIME 
*Situations that may have the potential to cause freefalling rod strings can include: 
 Engine or power train failure 
 Transmission disengaging 
 Transfer case disengaging 
 Hydraulic system failure 
 Hydraulic hose failure 
 Any type of suspected equipment failure. 
Should any of these occur, refer to the emergency response procedure as above.

4.19 EMERGENCY WELL BLOWOUT DRILL 
 
The purpose of a blowout drill is to ensure the readiness of the crew to detect a 
well kick and perform a shut in for the operation in progress. The cr ew should be 
capable of applying well control procedures both while tripping and when a rod 
and pump are out of the hole. Check the Weatherford COROD operations 
logbook to ensure that a routine drill is performed once every seven calendar 
days.  
 
The drill is to be initiated by the rig operator. The drill begins with the rig operator 
sounding an alert using a horn. The horn is to be a long and loud steady blast. A 
horn is the required method of alerting the crew. If there is cause to believe the 
horn on the company equipment is not operational, a small portable air horn is to 
be carried in the vehicle and used. 
 
Once an alert is raised, the crew should immediately take steps to shut in the 
well. If they are in the process of tripping, the crew should apply the brake, close 
the BOP and install a rod clamp. They then need to meet at an agreed upon 
muster point to discuss the next steps with the company representative. 
 
If adverse well conditions will not permit a “hands -on” drill, have the rig operator 
and/or the operator’s representative conduct a verbal drill on the lease. 
 
If the crew is not properly trained, the necessary training should be provided by 
the onsite supervisors. 
 
The operator must provide evidence that they are competent and qualified to 
perform a well shut in. They must possess a Well Service Blowout Prevention 
certificate or equivalent issued through a Weatherford recognized training 
program. If the operator does not have proof of this training, the rig can be shut 
down until such time as a qualified operator takes over operations.

4.20 H2S Emergency – Man Down Drill 
Each crew must have an Emergency Response Plan to ensure the safety of 
employees in the event of an emergency. Every COROD field service employee 
must be familiar with the emergency response plan. The “Man Down” drill is 
specifically intended to simulate a scenario in which a worker in the immediate 
area of the well is overcome by H2S gas and falls to the ground. 
 
Units with multi member crews are required to perform this drill at leas t once in 
every 30 day period. Individual operators such as Mobile Gripper operators 
should do everything possible including attempting to coordinate a similar drill 
with third party crews if nec essary to practice this important emergency 
procedure. Because It is unusual to work with other COROD crews and many 
third party crews may not have the same requirement as Weatherford, the Mobile 
Gripper operator is not required to log a Man Down exercise in a given schedule 
but you are strongly encouraged to make an effort to practice an H2S emergency. 
 
The drill should be discussed in the Pre -Job Safety Meeting and the important 
points of the drill should be covered so that everyone present is aware of th e 
necessary actions to take and informed of the designated muster point. It is best 
if the drill is performed with at least three people present. 
 
The drill should be performed at a point in the job when it is safe to do so. Do not 
initiate the drill at a critical point in the job or when a significant depth of rod string 
is being supported by the rig. The operator should pick a time when operations 
are going forward normally, but performing the drill will not create a greater 
likelihood of a genuine incident. 
 
To perform the Man Down Drill: 
 
1. The lead operator will indicate to a member of the crew that they are the “Man 
Down” and that person will immediately fall to the ground in the prone 
position. 
2. The operator will raise the alarm by sounding one long blas t using the rig’s air 
horn. 
3. In a genuine emergency, depending on the state of the job, the operator will 
activate the Safety and BOP inflation systems only if it is necessary and only 
if it is safe to do so. 
4. All workers on the lease other than the man down  will immediately evacuate 
to the designated muster point. 
5. Once assembled at the muster point each person working on the lease 
should be accounted for before attempting to rescue the man down.  
6. One or two workers must then be assigned to put on their Self Contained 
Breathing Apparatus and return to the work area to rescue the down man.

present possible variations of the emergency scenario which cannot be safely 
simulated.

4.21 ROD HANDLING 
 
Safety of both the worker and the COROD are of critical importance when 
handling the COROD. There are several situations in which either the COROD or 
the worker are in danger: 
 
1. Injector Spacing 
 
When tripping COROD into or out of the well, the spacing between the 
Injector and the BOP stack is critic al. If the spacing is too great and the 
COROD meets an obstruction  it is possible for the COROD to buckle. 
Once kinked, the bent  section must be removed and the COROD spliced 
together.  
 
2. Aligning the COROD in the Injector’s Gripper Chains 
 
When installing the Injector it is necessary to carefully align the COROD 
string in the gripper chains. Improper alignment can damage both the 
COROD and the Gripper Pads.  
 
In addition, the Injector represents a serious pinching/crushing hazard. No 
crew member should ever  place their hands between the pressure beams 
of the Injector. A tool exists specifically to align the COROD which is 
intended to keep the worker’s hands free of the Gripper Chains. 
 
3. Threading COROD into the Rod Guide 
 
When running the COROD into the rod g uide, either from a service reel or 
transport reel  or using the Injector  the leader cable is the preferred 
method. If manually pushing the COROD through the rod guide  it is 
possible to catch the COROD on one of the joints in the guide. This will 
cause damage to the guide and the COROD. Always ensure that a bullet 
is fastened to the end of the COROD before running it into the guide. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
CAUTION: 
 
ENSURE THE SET SCREW USED ON BLANK BULLETS DO NOT 
PROTUDE OR STICK OUT NOTICEABLY PAST THE BULLET. 
FAILURE TO DO THIS CAN LEAD TO THE SET SCREW GETTING 
CAUGHT CAUSING DAMAGE TO THE GUIDE AND ROD.

4. Removing COROD from a reel to be delivered by hand to the welding 
table 
 
When manually unraveling COROD from either a service reel or transport 
reel it is very important to ensure that only the required length of rod can 
escape the reel and the remainder of the COROD is carefully secured in 
the pocket of the reel. Each Transport Trailer must be equipped with (1) 
Trailer Safety Fork and each Field Welder with (2) Working Safety Forks 
as well as a third working fork on a long handle which allows the fork to be 
engaged at a distance. 
 
On transport reels the two Working Forks  must be used to continuously 
ensure that the COROD is contained in the pocket of the reel in at least 
one place. The Trailer Safety Fork must be used on Drive -in Splices and 
during transport. 
 
On service reels ensure that the COROD is tied securely to the reel in at 
least two, but preferably three l ocations after the necessary length of rod 
has been removed from the pocket.

6. Injecting COROD through the BOP stack 
 
When initially inserting the COROD through the BOP stack i nto the well, 
there are several points at which the COROD can be caught going 
through the stack. Before running the COROD into the well, ensure that 
the Rod Safety Clamp is open, the Rod Wiper is backed off and the Regan 
BOP is un-pressurized. 
 
When passing a large bore pump through the Rod Safety Clamp the 
backing plate may have to be removed. Ensure that it is replaced pri or to 
commencing work. 
 
7. Surfacing a COROD string 
 
When surfacing a COROD string it is important to attach the Rod 
Containment Device , at a point just before the rod exits the guide,  to 
prevent the COROD from leaving the g uide in a dangerous fashion. No 
worker should ever stand in the area near the Rod Guide mouth in case 
the Rod Containment Device malfunctions.

4.22  FIRE EXTINGUISHER USE AND INSPECTION 
 
Each Field Welder is equipped with two, ABC dry chemical fire extinguishers. 
This type of fire extinguisher is designed to fight flammable liquid fires as well as 
electrical fires. The majority of fire extinguishers in the COROD North American 
fleet are of the “Cartridge Operated” dry chemical type and are manufactured by 
Ansul. Other regions use “Stored Pressure” type dry chemical fire extinguishers.  
 
Regardless of the type of extinguisher used the basic steps used to put out a  fire 
are the same. The following acronym can be used to easily remember what to 
do. 
 
It is equally important that each operator carefully read the instructions listed on 
the side of the fire extinguisher on his/her unit. The fire extinguisher’s label will 
identify any special instructions applicable to that extinguisher.

Each month, as part of the Mobile Gripper’s  monthly inspection, the fire 
extinguishers must be inspected by the operator of the unit. This monthly 
inspection is critical to the ongoi ng functionality of the fire extinguisher and must 
be completed and recorded every month. The inspection procedure is outlined in 
detail in SOP MG 046 . Additional information regarding the fire extinguishers can 
be found in COROD Technical Bulletin #109 
 
The steps of the monthly inspection are as follows: 
 
 Ensure that the fire extinguishers are in their specified holders and not 
obstructed by any equipment or other objects. Holders should secure  the 
extinguisher when locked. 
 Ensure the safety pins are in place and tamper seal is intact on the pin. 
 Remove the extinguisher from its holder and clean out any collected debris or 
moisture. 
 Check to ensure that the tamper seals are intact on cartridges and pins. If 
tamper seals have been removed they must be replaced or the extinguisher 
must be taken out of service. 
 Check that pressure gauge is in the operable range on the dial (if applicable). 
 Ensure that there are no dents, leaks, rust, chemical deposi ts or other signs of 
damage/abuse. Wipe off any oil, chemicals or dirt/mud which may have landed 
on the extinguisher. 
 Ensure the annual inspection tags are in place. Record the Annual Certification 
date.  
 Check the hydro test date on the extinguishers. Rec ord the Hydrostatic testing 
date.  
 Document the monthly inspection in the COROD Operations Logbook. 
 
SHAKING OR OVERTURNING THE EXTINGUISHER TO “FLUFF” THE

POWDER IS TRAPPED IN CERTAIN SENSITIVE PARTS O F THE FIRE 
EXTINGUISHER. 
 
OPERATING THE NOZZLE ON CARTRIDGE OPERATED EXTINGUISHERS 
CAN EXPOSE THE DRY CHEMICAL TO HUMIDITY AND CAUSE CAKING. 
DO NOT OPERATE THE NOZZLE DURING THIS INSPECTION.
