4.23 WORKING IN EXTREME TEMPERATURES 
 
A worker who is Thermally Comfortable will not feel too hot or too cold. This is 
obviously the ideal. Some degree of thermal discomfort may be unavoidable and 
the worker can continue to work in these conditions without danger or risk of 
temperature related injury, however workers s hould be made as comfortable as 
possible to avoid accelerated fatigue and the increased likelihood of mistakes. 
 
4.23.1 Effects of Cold Weather on Personnel 
 
The ability of workers to function and work normally is affected by exposure to 
cold conditions. As the temperature decreases and the time of exposure 
increases many side effects can occur which are detrimental to health and 
safety.  
 
Table 2 shows the eff ect of wind speed on the effective temperature. Note that 
even at the reasonably warm winter temperature of -12º C (10º F) a high wind will 
bring the effective temperature below a safe value. 
 
Table 2: Wind Speed and Effective Temperature 
 
Table 3 shows recommendations for length of work period and the number 
of breaks in a four hour shift at temperatures below -25º C ( -13º F) and 
various wind chill values.  When the air temperature is below -29ºC (-20ºF) 
or an equal windchill exists, the site supervisor will decide whether to 
continue operations which involve working outside and implement 
appropriate precautions.

4.23.2 Effects of Cold Weather on Equipment 
Cold weather conditions often causes problems for equipment,  especially the 
hydraulic systems. Cold hydraulic fluid can cause damage 
to hydraulic components, slow operation of hydraulic 
functions and cause components to function 
unpredictably. This includes the pulling capacity of the 
hoisting system. The colder the ambient temperature, the 
less weight the pulling u nit is capable of lifting. Ensure 
that the engine pre -heater circuit is open to the hydraulic 
tank and that the hydraulic cooler bypass switch is in the 
“bypass” position in cold weather. 
 
 
Cold weather also has adverse affect  on the structural integrity of steel 
components. This includes the rig structure itself as well as items such as wire 
ropes for hoisting. Shane, I am waiting for reply from Hermann on the specific 
affect of cold weather on steel rating. 
4.23.3 Effects of Hot Weather on Personnel 
As temperatures rise, the worker may move into a condition of heat stress. In this 
condition the body is no longer able to regulate its own temperature. Heat stress 
is a potentially life threatening condition. Anyone working in or supervi sing 
workers in hot weather conditions should be trained to recognize the symptoms 
of heat stress.  Fluids should be located at or near where the work is being done. 
As a rough guide, workers working under hot conditions should drink 
approximately 250 ml (1 cup) of fluid every 20 minutes. 
 
Table 4 shows the effect of humidity on how hot the ambient temperature feels. 
This is called the Humidex and is used to determine the level of discomfort a 
worker is likely to feel and to judge worker safety in given con ditions. If the 
reading is in the yellow area or higher, certain types of outdoor work should be 
reduced or modified, depending on the age and health of the individual, their 
physical condition, the type of clothing worn, and other weather conditions. For 
workers who wear cotton overalls on top of warm weather clothing, the humidex 
reading should be increased by a correction factor of +5ºC (+9°F).

4.23.4 Heat Illness Prevention Planning 
 
Below are some signs and symptoms of heat stress and heat stroke as well as 
steps that can be taken should such signs arise.  
 
Symptoms - When the body is unable to cool itself by sweating, several heat -
induced illnesses such as heat stress or heat exhaust ion and the more severe 
heat stroke can occur, and can result in death. 
 
Factors Leading to Heat Stress - High temperature and humidity; direct sun or 
heat; limited air movement; physical exertion; poor physical condition; some 
medicines; and inadequate tolerance for hot workplaces. 
 
Level 1 - Symptoms of Heat Exhaustion  
Headaches, dizziness, lightheadedness or fainting. / Weakness and moist skin. / 
Mood changes such as irritability or confusion. / Upset stomach or vomiting. 
 
Level 2 - Symptoms of Heat Stroke 
Dry, hot skin with no sweating. / Mental confusion or losing consciousness. / 
Seizures or convulsions.

provide extra water

Emergency Procedures 
 
Level 1 – Heat Stress 
 
o Notify Supervisor and/or HSSE immediately.  
o Contact local EMS. 
o Move the worker to a cool, shaded area. 
o Loosen or remove heavy clothing. 
o Provide cool drinking water. 
 
Level 2 – Heat Stoke 
 
a. Notify Supervisor and/or HSSE immediately.  
b. Contact local EMS 
c. Move the worker to a cool, shaded or air conditioned area. 
d. Place the feet higher than the head to avoid shock 
e. Loosen or remove heavy clothing 
f. Apply cold compress to arm pits, neck and groin area 
g. Wrap the employee in wet cloths and fan  
h. Have the person drink cool water, if they are able. 
i. If possible have other workers meet and direct emergency care 
providers 
 
4.23.5 Effects of Hot Weather on Equipment 
 
In hot weather it is important to maintain a consistent safe temperature in the 
hydraulic system. Check the temperature of the hydraulic reservoir regularly in 
warm weather. Make certain the cooling bypass is closed to achieve maximum 
cooling. Ensure the tank heater is shut down during warm weather operations. To 
facilitate faster cooling of the hydraulic fluid, clamp 
off the COROD string if necessary and allow the 
mobile equipment’s pumps to turn without load. 
This will allow fluid to pass through the cool er 
without heating up in the hydraulic system. 
Always pay attention to the hydraulic fluid 
temperature in warm weather and make sure 
that the cooler fan is running at the 
appropriate fluid temperatures.

4.24 LIGHTNING SAFETY 
 
There are times when working on a  lease during a rain storm that lightning may 
be present. Thunderstorms can move in quickly and workers must remain aware 
of changing weather conditions. 
 
Although Corigs™ are grounded much of the exposed surface that workers are in 
contact with is metal. Lightning discharges do not always follow a straight path 
into the ground. The lightning can fan out along the surface of the ground as well, 
which is dangerous for anyone working at the time of the strike. The workers 
need to get as far away from touching  the metal surfaces as possible. If they stay 
near the rig they can become a conductor along with the rig. 
 
The safest advice is to stop working on the Corig™ and seek shelter inside the 
crew truck or the dog house.   
 
A rule of thumb for seeking shelter a nd remaining there is the 30 -30 rule. If the 
worker is able to count to less than 30 between lightning strikes, they need to 
leave the rig as it is and seek shelter. After getting into the crew truck or 
doghouse, remain there for 30 minutes after the last thunder.

4.25 EQUIPMENT CERTIFICATIONS 
 
Many components and tools on the Mobile Gripper require periodic inspection 
and recertification. Inspection of these items must be logged in the Mobile 
Gripper Operations Logbook and certifying documents must be available for 
review on the unit at all times.  
4.25.1 Knuckle Picker 
As per the Weatherford Global Safety Manual  the crane must be inspected and 
recertified annually by a P rofessional Engineer or the manufacturer’s agent. It is 
Weatherford’s practice to have the OEM agent inspect the crane. The crane must 
also receive level I and II inspections on a daily basis and the results of these 
inspections must be recorded in the MG’s logbook along with the OEM 
recertification documentation.  
 
4.25.2 Rod Elevators , Lift Mandrel, Shackles, Wire Ropes and other lifting 
apparatus 
Overhead lifting equipment includes such items as  rod hooks, rod elevators and 
slings and shackles . Overhead lifting equipment is inspected and recertified 
based on the schedule outlined in the COROD Level III Overhead Lifting 
Equipment Inspection Manual. Ensure that all levels of inspection are recorded in 
the Weatherford COROD Operations logbook and that the level IV recertifica tion 
documents are stored in the log book. 
4.25.3 BOP and BOP Hoses 
The BOP and its hoses must be recertified every three years by the Original 
Equipment Manufacturer or equivalent.

According to Section 4.7 of the Weatherford Global Safety Ma nual there are two

hydrostatic test which must be performed every six years. The fire extinguishers 
are not accompanied by any documentation, however on each monthly 
inspection the annual and hydro-test recertification dates must be recorded in the 
MG’s Corod Operations L og Book and a detailed inspection of the extinguisher 
and its components must be performed. 
 
4.25.5 Wellhead Flanges 
Wellhead flanges are certified at the time o f manufacture to meet the 
requirements of the API standard 6A. This certification document must be 
available for review on the unit at all times. 
 
4.25.6 Mobile Gripper Deck Inspection 
The Mobile Gripper Deck and all of its components including, but not limited to 
the Mobile Injector and its lifting apparatus, BOP stack and its lifting lugs, Rod 
Guide handles and pivot lugs  and Rod Safety Clamp  must be inspected daily in 
Level 1 and 2 inspections. In addition the Deck and its components must 
undergo a through monthly inspection and Annual recertification.

4.26 SAFE USE OF HAND TOOLS 
 
Refer to Section 27 of the Weatherford Global Safety Manual for more 
information regarding the safe use of hand tools. 
4.26.1 Using a Hammer 
 
The Sledge Hammer is most commonly used by shop and field service 
personnel. The handle should never be used for  prying or pounding and the 
hammer should be discarded if the handle is loose or damaged in any way. 
 
Always grip a hammer close to the end of the handle to increase leverage. 
Whenever possible, strike the object squarely  with the full face of the head to 
prevent damage to the hammer face and the object.  When using a Sledge, lift 
with the hammer close to the body and allow the weight of the head to do the 
work.

4.26.2 Using a Hacksaw 
 
 
Hacksaw frames are designed to receive blades of varying lengths. Make sure 
when placing a blade in the frame that the teeth are pointing away from the 
handle and the blade is stretched tightly. Also ensure that sufficient adjustment is 
left to tighten the blade as it becomes loose.  
 
Select the correct blade for the material to be sawn by ensuring that there are 
always at least two teeth on the material at all  times. Except when starting a cut, 
always use the full length of the blade on every stroke.

4.26.3 Using a Screw Driver 
Screwdrivers are the tool most easily misused.  Never use a Screwdriver as a 
cold chisel, a punch or a prying bar as this will damage the tool. Screwdrivers 
should only be used to install and remove threaded fasteners.  
 
Keep handles clean and free of grease and discard Screwdrivers with damaged 
or broken handles. Likewise, Screwdrivers with damaged or broken tips should 
be repaired or discarded. Damaged tips cause damage to the fasteners they are 
used on.

4.26.4 Crescent Wrenches 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Adjustable Wrenches are  not intended for hard service ; treat them gently. 
Adjustable wrenches are also not intended to take the place of an open end 
wrench except when an odd sized nut is encountered. 
 
Always place the adjustable wrench on the nut so that the pulling force is applied 
to the stationary jaw. Tighten the adjusting screw so that the wrench fits the nut 
snugly. Always pull any wrench toward the body rather than pushing it away.

4.26.5 Using Open End Wrenches 
 
The size of a wrench refers to the distance across the flats  of the nut or bolt for 
which it is intended and not the bolt diameter. Ensure that the correct size 
wrench is used to prevent damage to the wrench and the fastener. 
 
The angled head of the wrench 
allows more sweep in tight  spaces 
by flipping the wrench.

4.26.6 Using a Pipe Wrench 
 
When using a pipe wrench a gap must be maintained between the pipe and the 
shank of the hook jaw. This allows the biting surface of the two jaws to create the 
gripping force of the wrench. Allowing the shank to contact the work may cause 
the wrench to slip and may cause the shank of the hook jaw to fail. 
 
The jam nut should not be at the very 
end of the hook jaw. If this is the case, 
move up to a larger wrench.

4.26.7 Using a Rod Wrench 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
When using a Rod Wrench it is important that the jaw of the wrench be fully 
engaged with the sucker rod or pony rod joint. When using snipes, ensure that 
the jaw of the wrench is not accidentally pushed off when the snipe is installed. 
 
 
4.26.8 Using a Ratchet and Socket 
 
The Ratchet handle allows the direction of tightening to be switched. Select the 
size of socket that fits the nut, engage it on the handle and place the socket on 
the nut.

objects. For very large objects use Channel Lok pliers.

4.26.10 Using Vise Grips 
 
Vise Grips are intended to be used for clamping round objects and should not be 
used on where marring the finish on the work would be a problem, for example 
the polished rod. Neither Vise Grips, nor any type of pliers, should be used for 
tightening or loosening nuts or bolts.

4.26.11 Using a File 
 
 
Normally push the file across the work, cutting only on the forward stroke. To 
prevent damage to the teeth, raise the file from the work on the return stroke. 
When finishing a flat surface, like a gasket surface, it should be draw filed. To 
draw-file, use a machinist’s file and draw it across the work crosswise applying 
only light pressure. This allows the file to cut in both directions. 
 
Never hammer on a file or use it for prying. Do not use a file whose teeth have 
been clogged. Clean the file with a file  card. Before using any file, always equip it 
with a tight fitting handle to eliminate the danger of injuring your hand.

4.26.12 Using a Chisel 
 
 
 
Chisels are used to cut metal in jobs such as breaking rivets and splitting nuts. 
The chisel should be held between the thumb and forefinger about an inch below 
the head of the chisel. Hold the tool with a somewhat loose grip to lessen the 
blow on the hand in case of a miss. 
 
 
 
 
The shape of the chisel head and edge are important to the safe use and 
performance of the chisel. When the edge becomes dull or the head becomes 
mushroomed, it must be reshaped with a grinding wheel. When grinding, avoid 
holding the chisel against eh grin ding wheel for long periods as the heat 
generated with draw the temper from the metal, rendering the chisel useless.

4.26.13 Using a Dog Collar 
 
If there is no handling pony between the pump rotor and the COROD string, a 
Dog Collar must be used to secure the rotor in the well while making or breaki ng 
the connection to the string. 
 
The Dog Collar consists of a series of segments each containing a toothed pad 
similar to the slips in a fishing tool.  The 
one shown in Figure 69 is similar to the 
Dog Collars used by Corod crews.  
 
The Dog Collar must be installed so that 
the slips are each solidly in co ntact with 
the rotor and tightened with an 18” pipe 
wrench. It is preferable to install the Dog 
Collar directly below some s ort of upset 
on the rotor to ensure that the rotor 
cannot fall.  
 
 
4.26.14 Using Snipes 
 
When using snipes to make or break a threaded connection, observe the 
following guidelines to ensure the safety of tools, COROD products and 
personnel: 
 
 Never use snipes on aluminum wrenches. The aluminum material is 
not strong enough and will be damaged b y the added leverage of the 
snipe. 
 
 Never use a snipe which is more than 2 times longer than the wrench 
to which it is applied. Too much applied force will damage even steel 
tools. 
 
 Always pull the snipe towards the body. Never push the snipe to apply 
torque.

4.26.15 Using the Polish Rod Wheel 
 
Some COROD strings are connected to the well’s down -hole pump with an 
“On/Off Tool” rather than a threaded coup ling. This tool style connection allows 
the COROD string to be disengaged from the down -hole pump and surfaced 
without surfacing the pump. There are many types of “On/Off” tools with different 
engagement and disengagement instructions, but in general a Pol ish Rod Wheel 
is required to rotate the COROD string to disengage the tool. 
 
The wheel is only to be used on the Polish Rod. Install the wheel by tightening 
the two Huber Polish Rod clamps to approximately 300lb -ft torque. When using 
the Polish Rod Wheel i t is important to note that a significant amount of torque is 
created in the COROD string. It is possible for the wheel to snap back if not used 
carefully creating a serious danger of hand injury. 
 
The other common use for the Polish Rod Wheel is during a Fishing Job. Many 
times, when fishing a parted rod, the fishing string must be manipulated to cause 
the fishing tool to latch onto the parted rod.

4.27 OTHER TOOLS 
4.27.1 Angle Finder 
 
The angle finder is a simple tool that is used to determine the angle of the 
wellhead. 
 
Place the magnetic base of the angle finder on the wellhead and read the angle 
off the dial. 
 
Once the angle of the wellhead is determined, the angle finder can be used to set 
the angle of equipment or other tools which must be lifted into position on or 
relative to the well head.

4.27.3 Polish Rod Wrench 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The polish rod wrench (a.k.a. Gear wrench, Tong wrench or Parmelee wrench) is 
designed for use on highly polished pipe or couplings and for working in close 
quarters. 
  
Features include: 
 
 The wrench has no teeth, so it cannot bite into and damage the pipe. 
 Sure and certain grip from the girth that completely surrounds the pipe. 
 Ideal for close quarter work where limited clearance does not permit the 
use of other types of wrenches. 
 Handy ratchet action of the wrench gives a new grip with each pull of the 
handle. 
 Handle, girth and lock bolt are made from drop forged steel, toughened 
and hardened by scientific heat treatment. 
 Separate girth is necessary for each diameter of pipe and cannot be used 
on diameters other than that for which it was made.

4.27.4 Rod Depth Counter (Depthometer) – Removable  
 
This tool is installed to track the length of 
COROD being installed or pulled from a well. 
There are two arms attached to the counting 
wheel. One arm is a s pring loaded tensioner 
arm that can be pulled back for ease of 
installation or removal. 
 
A chain is slung through the handle and then 
secured to a point above the well, preferably 
on the mast. As the rod moves it rotates the 
wheel which then displays the distance near 
the handle. 
 
 
 
 
 
4.27.5 Rod Depth Counter – Mounted Mechanical Rod Counter 
 
 
A mechanical rod counter is mounted on some pulling units and is considered 
part of the pulling unit. A gear mounted on the face of the pulling unit body is 
engaged by the gripper chains. That gear drives a mechanical counter which is 
mounted on a bracket below the rod straightener via a long drive shaft. 
 
Length may be displayed in either Metric or 
Imperial units. Be c ertain you are familiar with 
the counter you are working with.

4.27.6 Ratchet Straps 
 
Ratchet Straps are used as a method of primary 
securement. They consist of two major components. 
There is a ratchet locking mechanism and a strap is 
secured through the ratchet. Each opposing end will 
have a rated hook.   
 
 
 
 
 
1. The free end of the strap is fed through the slot in the drum on the ratchet. 
 
2. The strap is pulled so that the strap is almost tight. Some slack must be 
left in order for the ratchet to secure the strap. 
 
3. While holding the ratchet in place, crank the handle back and forth until 
the strap is secured.  
 
4. To release, pull the latch toward the handle and pull the ratchet assembly.

4.27.7 Slings 
Performing overhead lifts with nylon and wire rope slings has significant risk 
associated with it. Proper rigging procedures should always be followed and the 
condition of the rigging equipment should be carefully assessed before any lift is 
attempted.  
 
Before using any slings, ensure all slings have rated capacity tags. Visually 
inspect slings for signs of damage. For further information on periodic testing, 
please refer to section 23.13 of the Weatherford Global Safety Manual. 
 
 Ensure that slings are hitched in a manner providing control of the load,  
 Ensure that sharp edges in contact with slings are padded with material of 
sufficient strength to protect the sling,  
 Ensure that slings are shortened or adjusted only by methods approved by 
the sling manufacturer or a qualified person,  
 Ensure that, during lifting with or without a load, personnel are alert for 
possible snagging,  
 Ensure that, in a basket hitch, the load is balanced to prevent slippage,  
 When using a basket hitch, ensure that the legs of the sling contain or 
support the load from the sides, above the center of gravity, so that the 
load remains under control,  
 Do not drag slings on the floor or over abrasive surfaces,  
 Ensure that, in a choker hitch, the choke point is only on the sling body, 
never on a splice or fitting,  
 Ensure that, in a choker hitch, an angle of choke less than 120 degrees is 
not used without reducing the rated load,  
 Ensure that slings are not constricted, bunched, or pinched by the load, 
hook, or any fitting,  
 Ensure that the load applied to the hook is centered in the base (bowl) of 
the hook to prevent point loading on the hook, unless the hook is designed 
for point loading,  
 Ensure that an object in the eye of a sling is not wider than one -third the 
length of the eye,  
 Do not shorten or lengthen a sling by knotting or twisting,  
 Do not rest loads on the sling,  
 Do not pull a sling from under a load when the load is resting on the sling,  
 Do not allow shock loading, and  
 Avoid twisting and kinking.  
 
 
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18

4.27.7.1 Nylon Slings 
Do not use synthetic web slings with loads in excess of the rated load capacities. 
Ensure that synthetic web slings have suitable characteristics for the type of load, 
hitch, and environment in which they will be used and that they are not used with 
loads in excess of the rated load capacities 
 
Nylon slings shall be removed from service if any defects such as the 
following are visible:  
 Acid or alkali burns  
 Melting, charring, or weld spatter on any part of the sling  
 Holes, tears, cuts, snags or embedded particles  
 Broken or worn stitching in load bearing splices  
 Excessive abrasive wear  
 Knots in any part of the sling  
 Distortion and excessive pitting or corrosion or broken fittings  
 Other apparent defects which cause doubt as to the strength of the 
sling  
 If sling rated capacity or sling  material identification is missing or not 
readable

4.27.7.2 Wire Rope Slings 
Wire rope slings shall be removed from service if any defects such as the 
following are visible and exceed minimum recommended standards as 
indicated:  
 Broken wires 
 Abrasion 
 Stretch  
 Crushed Strands  
 Corrosion 
 Bad end attachments 
 If sling rated capacity or sling material identification is missing or not 
readable 
4.27.8 Shackles 
 
Do not attempt lifting operations unless you understand the use of the equipment 
and the slinging procedures. 
 
 Do not use defective shackles or unidentified pins. 
 
 Shackles should be fitted so that the body takes the load along its centre 
line and is not subjected to side bending loads. When connecting a 
number of sling legs position them so that they do not impose a side load 
on the shackle jaws. Use spacers to position them if necessary. 
 
 Ensure the pin is correctly screwed into the shackle eye. Tighten by hand, 
use a small bar to lock the collar to the shackle eye. Check that the thread 
is fully engaged with the body but is not too long so that tightening causes 
the body to deform. 
 
 With bolt and nut pins ensure the nut jams on the inner end of the thread 
and not on the eye of the shackle. The bolt should be free to rotate with 
minimal side float. The split cotter pin must be fitted before making a lift. 
 
 When using shackles with slings in choke hitch, or in other applications 
there may be movement, place the pin through the eye or link of the sling 
and never in contact with the bight of the choke or moving parts which 
may cause the pin to unscrew.

4.27.9 Reciprocating Pump Collapsing Tool 
 
When removing or installing reciprocating pumps from wells for service or 
replacement, one of the methods used is to make use of a pump collapsing tool. 
This tool is used to slide through the pump in order to hold the pump in place 
while the crew breaks the pump connection with the rod.   
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
When preparing the pump collapsing tool, 
remove the hook and pin connection and insert 
through eye end of sling. Reinstall the hook and 
pin connection. Insert a wire through the pin end 
and twist shut to ensure the pin does not back 
out during operation. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
When pulling a pump from ser vice, after the rod 
connection has been broken, the rod elevator will 
be hanging the pony rod. The pump will be lifted 
up enough to insert the pump collapsing tool 
through the slotted opening of the pump.

4.27.10 Rod Elevators – Use and Selection 
Rod elevators are the tools used to physically manipulate COROD and other well 
components into and out of a well. The bail of the elevator is designed to fit in the 
rod hook and the body of the elevator is sized to match the upset of a standard 
API pin end or polish rod coupling. The elevator has spring loaded latches which 
positively lock it onto the rod, once locked in the string can then be lifted out of 
the well. 
 
A Level 1 inspection must be performed on rod elevators before each use 
(Section 3.9.2). This is a visual inspection of the rod elevator and components 
(bail, latches, springs, plates). See Section 5.2.3.6 for more information on how 
to inspect rod elevators.  
 
Rod elevators are stamped with the rod size that they are intended for. The size 
of the elevators must match the size of the rod being handled.  
Table 7 contains a list of elevators and their compatibility with different rod sizes 
and pin ends.

There are two main types of Rod Elevator. Sucker Rod Elevators are used on

which is beveled to  match the shape of the upset on the pin end. Sucker Rod 
Elevators are usually applicable to more than one standard API rod size however 
a variety of elevators must be kept on hand to accommodate all of the sizes of 
rod which are encountered in the field. 
 
Polish Rod Elevators are manufactured specifically for use on Polish Rods and 
have a square shoulder at the center of the body to mate with the square 
shoulder of a Polish Rod Coupling. Polish Rod Elevators are engineered to fit 
over either a 1 -1/4” or 1 -1/2” polish rod and do not have a replaceable plate in 
the body.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
CAUTION: 
 
USING A POLISH ROD ELEVATOR ON A STANDARD PIN END 
OR A SUCKER ROD ELEVATOR ON A POLISH ROD COUPLING 
WILL CAUSE DAMAGE TO BOTH THE ELEVATOR AND THE 
ROD. ALWAYS USE THE CORRECT ELEVATOR FOR THE 
APPLICATION AT HAND.

4.27.11 Rod Calibration Tool (The Y-Tool) 
 
When round COROD has been in a well that has been operating either a rotating 
progressing cavity pump (PCP) or a reciprocating rod pump (RRP), particularly 
on slant or deviated wells, there may be areas of wear that occur between the 
COROD and the production tubing. The R od Calibration Tool (or Y -Tool) 
measures the amount of wear on the round COROD as it passes through the 
head of the tool. The tool can mounted either on the well or a special bracket 
below the mast and consists of a Measurement Head and a digital LCD Displ ay 
unit which shows values such as rod size, depth and rate of assent/decent. 
 
Power from the deck electrical system (12 
VDC at 1.2 Amps) is supplied through a 
fused and switched circuit in the main 
electrical panel. There is an additional fast 
blowing 3 Amp fuse located in the Y -Tool’s 
electrical junction box located on the rig’s 
drivers side and about 3 m (10 feet) from the 
operators station. Sufficient cable is supplied 
to reach from the Y -Tool electrical junction 
box to the wellhead. 
 
 
 
4.27.11.1 The Measurement Head 
 
The “Y” shaped measurement head measures the diameter of round COROD in 
both the X and Y axes as it passes through the unit. Round rod ranging from 
0.750” to 1.160” in diameter can be 
measured with an accuracy of ± 0.020”. 
The “Y” tool would typically sit on the top of 
the rod stripper or BOP assembly and may 
require the use of the  BOP plate for 
support. 
 
The Y -Tool is not designed to measure 
Elliptical COROD.

4.27.11.2 The Optical Encoder 
 
A sprocket driven optical encoder senses chain movement in either direction and 
measures the d istance travelled by the gripper chains as they injector or retrieve 
COROD. The optical encoder is housed in a sealed 
assembly mounted on the face of the pulling unit 
and is activated by chain movement. The encoder 
receives its power from the portable LCD Display 
unit. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
4.27.11.3 The Portable LCD Display 
 
A portable LCD Display unit with audible and visible limit alarms displays the 
measured data. The LCD display unit houses the power supply and the power 
regulators for the optical encoder and measurement head. The X and Y values of 
rod diameter are display ed on the top line of the disply. The depth of the rod is 
shown on the bottom line of the display.

The display unit is programmed to alert the operator when it measures a rod 
diameter less than 0.900” in either axis, regardless of the size of the rod being 
measured. The unit can also display the depth, the rate at which the rod is 
traveling and the hydraulic tank temperature in either English or Metric units. 
 
When worn COROD passes through the Y -Tool measurement head assembly 
and a measurement is taken which is below the minimum limit in either the X or Y 
axis both the audible and visible alarms will be triggered. The alarms will 
continue until the operator resets the unit by pressing button #3.  
 
Button #3 will reset the unit and cancel the alarms but only if the tool has moved 
to a section of rod which measures above the minimum limit in both axes. As 
long as either axis of measurement is below the threshold d iameter the alarm will 
continue to sound and cannot be reset. 
 
Both axes of diameter are being measured at all times when the measurement 
tool is in place regardless of the display option chosen. Even when the operator 
has selected the Rate or Temperature functions, if the Y -Tool measures a 
section of rod below the minimum diameter value the audible and visible alarms 
will still sound. 
 
4.27.11.4 The Nitrogen Gas System 
 
The sensors within the measurement head which measure the rod as it passes 
are actually small ret ractable rollers. Pressurized Nitrogen gas is used to extend 
and retract the rollers. The use of Nitrogen gas from a sealed container also 
ensures that the Y-Tool will not be affected by freezing moisture in cold weather. 
In addition, the natural leakage o f Nitrogen within the measurement head purges 
the unit of other gases and ensures that no combustible fumes from the well can 
enter the unit and come in contact with the electrical circuits. 
 
A custom regulator is used on the Nitrogen system 
to reduce pressure to between 55 and 65 psig. This 
is adequate pressure to ensure that the rollers 
remain clamped on the COROD string as it travels 
through the head.

4.27.11.5 Y-Tool Controls 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Button #1  Toggles between displaying dimensions, rate or hydraulic temperature 
on the top line of the LCD display. 
 
Button #2 Toggles between displaying values in English units, or Metric units. 
 
Button #3  Toggles the top line of the LCD display between rate and hydraulic 
tank temperature. Also serves as a RESET button for the audible and visual  
alarms if the alarms have been activated. 
 
Button #4  allows some adjustment in the contrast of the LCD Display, useful on 
very cold days. 
 
Buttons #1 and #4  pushed together at the same time and held for 10 seconds 
will reset the depth display to “0.0”. 
 
The volume of the audible alarm can be adjusted by rotating the face of the 
speaker.

4.27.11.6 Y-Tool Operation 
 
 Install pressure gauge onto nitrogen bottles (Nitrogen Gas Regulator come 
with two gauges, no separate gauge installation is required) 
 Check nitrogen bottle bracket to make sure bottles are secure. (Check 
daily) 
 Check Nitrogen Gas Regulator before installing  to nitrogen bottle, for 
cracked lens or damaged threads. 
 
 Install electrical cables onto Y-Tool junction box 
 Ensure the Y -Tool power switch on main electrical panel is in OFF 
position bef ore hooking up electrical cables (power on may cause 
damage to unit) 
 Check all cables for cracks or damage to plug in connections.(clean 
daily) 
 Connect Optical Encoder cable to LCD Display 
 Install cables from junction box to measurement head and display unit 
 
 Install nitrogen hoses to measurement head 
 Check hoses for damage; insure quick couplers are free from dirt or 
debris 
 Test the direction of the roller actuators by operating the open/close 
valve The Lines should be made up so that there is only one way  to 
install them. 
 
 Turn on nitrogen to hoses 
1. Turn on nitrogen, check for leaks at all connections. 
2. Adjust gas pressure to approx 354 to 418 kPa (55 to 65 psi) 
3. Install measurement head on COROD. Pull control knob to close unit. 
(Keep fingers away from pinch points).

 Turning on power to Y-Tool by flipping the switch on the main electrical panel 
 
 When job is finished 
 turn main power switch to OFF 
 Turn off gas at nitrogen bottles. 
 Bleed system off by means of control knob at back of rig. 
 Disconnect all hoses (replace all dust caps on hoses and at the 
nitrogen bottles) 
 Disconnect all electrical cables, clean all connectors, install caps on 
ends of cables and at control panel.  
 Remove counter from back of rig and replace in carrying case 
 Clean and store equipment in doghouse. 
 
 
4.27.11.7 Tips for Effective use of the Y-Tool 
 
The optical encoder which counts the length of rod which has been moved 
considers downward motion of the chains as negative displacement and upward 
motion as positive displacement of the rod. So when performing an installation, if 
the unit is set to zero as the bottom pin enters the BOP, the LCD display will 
show the final depth of the string as a negative value. 
 
Likewise, if the counter is set to zero at the beginning of a Surface job, t he final 
string length when the pump reaches the surface will be shown as a positive 
value. 
NOTE 
 
DO NOT SECURE THE TOOL TO THE WELLHEAD WITH 
WIRES OR ROPE. THE CALIBRATING HEAD NEEDS THE 
ABILITY TO MOVE AS WELL AS SPACE TO RISE OFF THE 
WELLHEAD WHEN THE SHEAR COMES THROUGH THE BOP. 
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18 

===== PAGE 139 =====
 
 
COROD® 
Continuous R od & Well Services 
 
© 2015 Weatherford International, Inc. 
This document is copy righted and, whether patentable or non -patentable subject matter, embodies the proprietary  and conf idential inf ormation of  
Weatherf ord. Recipients agree it is loaned in conf idence with the understanding that neither it , nor the inf ormation in it, will be reproduced, used or di sclos ed ,  
in whole nor in part f or any  purpose except the limited purpose f or which it is loaned. This document shall be returned to We atherf ord upon de mand. 
 
139 
 
For the best accuracy of depth measurements when tripping in hole, the display 
should be zeroes just as the bottom pin goes into the BOP. Doing this will 
reference all depth measurements to the BOP on the wellhead. 
 
When performing a Surface, the COROD string is lifted into the pulling unit using 
the main blocks and so the counter will not register the length of rod above the 
pulling unit. To ensure the best accurac y when measuring string length while 
tripping out of hole, activate the Y -Tool and rotate the gripper chains up until a 
rod length equal to that already pulled from the well is displayed (typically about 
6m or 19.6ft) to account for this unmeasured section  of COROD. Then install the 
COROD in the pulling unit, put the measuring tool in place and continue to 
surface the string normally.  
 
When tripping rod, if an alarm does occur the rig operator should mark the 
location and record the depth of the worn area from the top of the string.  
Typically the operator would write down the location of the begiing and the 
ending of the worn area. Once surfaced, the final string length must also be 
recorded.  
 
The crew that comes in to repair the rod will have to work from  the bottom pin, 
repairing the deepest sections of worn rod first. If the surfacing crew recorded 
that the section of the string from 450m to 480m must be removed and the 
overall length of the string is 800m, the repairing crew will subtract 480m from the 
overall length of 800m and begin their splice at 320m from the bottom pin. 
 
General Notes: 
Upon first powering up the Display, buttons #1, #2, and #3 will require one push 
to initiate the toggle action. 
 
It is recommended that after each job, the counter s ystem be zeroed before 
dismantling the system. 
 
It is best to make all the electrical connections BEFORE powering up the system. 
 
The gas should be turned on AFTER the hoses have been connected to the Y -
Tool.

4.27.11.9 Y-Tool Care and Maintenance 
 
The Y-Tool head is to be kept in a secure location and not exposed to the 
elements. Typically it is kept in the doghouse, with the display kept in an ABS 
plastic Pelican Instrument case,  either in the cab of the Mobile Gripper  (initially 
shipped in a Yellow 1400 Pelican case). 
 
LPS Electro 140 Contact Cleaner, Part Number 00916, JDE Number: 514110 is 
the specified safe cleaning solvent for the purpose of cleaning the Y -Tool 
equipment. http://www.lpslabs.com/product_pg/cleaners_pg/Electro140.html  
 
When the equipment is not in use, the dust caps should be used to prevent 
moisture and dirt from entering the connector ends. 
 
On completion of the last job of the day, the cable ends should be cleaned with a 
small amount of LPS Contact Cleaner before applying the dust caps. It is 
important to keep the threaded parts of the connectors clean and lubricated, so 
that they will mate properly. 
 
The LPS Contact Cleaner has been tested and is safe to use on the plastic lens 
of the LCD Display unit. DO NOT use “Brake Kleen” to clean the Display unit, as 
it will etch the lens. 
 
After use, the Y -Tool head is typically covered in part by crude oil and possibly 
diesel fuel. The Y tool should be wiped with a clean  rag, and the bearing area 
brushed and wiped with “Varsol” or similar chemical to remove any residual 
crude oil. 
 
 
 
NOTE 
 
THE Y -TOOL EQUIPMENT WAS NOT DESIGNED TO BE K EPT

4.28 RELIEVING HYDRAULIC PRESSURE 
 
Incidents have occurred in which technicians and operators have been injured by 
residual pressure when attempting to service hydraulic components on mobile 
equipment.  
 
To ensure that there is no pressure in the Mobile Gripper’s hydraulic system: 
 
1. Disengage the hydraulic pump and shut the truck engine down. 
 
2. Open the Safety and Pressure Beam Bleed Valves on the operator’s 
console to bleed system pressure from both systems. 
 
3. Operate the Safety and Pressure Beam circuits to use up any residual 
pressure stored in the accumulators.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
WARNING: 
 
SERIOUS INJURY CAN OCCUR IF HYDRAULIC 
PRESSURE IS NOT RELIEVED PRIOR TO 
PERFORMING SERVICE ON THE UNIT’S 
HYDRAULIC SYSTEM. REFER TO COROD 
CRITICAL PROCEDURE #23 FOR INFORMATION 
ON RELIEVING PRESSURE ON THIS AND  
OTHER TYPES OF COROD MOBILE EQUIPMENT.

4.29 SECURING THE WORK SITE 
 
There may be an occasion where the work needs to momentarily stop with 
COROD in the wellbore. This could be due to: investigating an issue with the 
Mobile Gripper ; interacting with customer representatives or simply taking a 
break. Whatever the reason may be, during these times it is extremely urgent 
that the well remains under control if no personnel are nearby. 
 
Do NOT leave the work area unattended without performing the following steps: 
 
 Ensure the BOP assembly is installed and the top plate is in place.  
 
 Install the correct sized rod clamp. 
 
 Bump test the rod clamp connection three times. With the rod still 
secure in the pulling unit or rod elevators, this entails lifting a nd 
dropping the rod clamp connection onto the BOP top plate. Make 
certain that there is no movement in the rod. If there is movement, 
tighten the rod clamp and install a second rod clamp immediately 
above the first clamp and bump test three times again. 
 
 Close the BOP assembly.

5 RIG UP OPERATIONS 
5.1 COMMON JOBS AND CONFIGURATIONS 
Each of the most common jobs requires certain specific considerations be made 
when spotting equipment and rigging up. The orientation of the Injector and BOP 
stack are also affected by the  type of rod in the well. The three common jobs 
performed by a Mobile Gripper on a day to day basis are:  
 
 The installation of a new string of COROD - referred to as an “Install” 
 Removal of a COROD string for the purpose of servicing a pump or other 
part of the well – referred to as a “Surface” 
 The reinstallation of a previously surfaced string after the well has been 
serviced – referred to as a “Rerun” 
 
Also common, but not necessarily a “daily” oc currence is a Parted Rod or 
“Fishing” job. This is done whenever the COROD string breaks in the well and 
must be retrieved or “Fished” from the well. A Parted Rod job is a fairly involved 
process. Assembly of the Fishing tool is described later in this sec tion and the full 
procedure is explained in detail, along with the other more common jobs, in the 
appendices. 
 
When surfacing a pump, the service crew will first kill the well, remove the pump 
drive unit and polish rod and connect a 6’ pony rod to the top pin of the COROD. 
Before installing the Injector, the top coupling of the COROD string must be lifted 
to a height of at least 7 feet above the top of the BOP stack to allow the Injector 
to be placed on a continuous section of the COROD. 
 
When performing an Install or Rerun, the service rig crew will first ensure that the 
well has been ki lled and remove the cap. The BOP  stack must be installed prior 
to anything being run into the well. The pump rotor is dropped through the BOP 
and the shear coupling connected and supported by the BOP Plate. The COROD 
can then be drawn through the Rod Guide and connected to the shear pin.  
 
These procedures will be illustrated further within the applicable sections 
upcoming in this manual. 
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18

5.2 RIG ENGINE WARM UP AND WALK AROUND 
5.2.1 Engine Warm-Up Guidelines 
The Mobile Gripper is equipped with a diesel fired, circulating type engine pre-
heating device (Webasto or ProHeat En gine Pre-Heater described in section 
2.2.7). At temperatures below 0 ºC (-32ºF) it is recommended that the engine pre -
heater be used to prevent damage to the rig engine during cold starting. During 
the summer months the engine pre -heater should be operated weekly to ensure 
that it is in working order and to burn off any soot. 
 
 
Prior to starting the rig engine at the beginning of each day, the engine oil and 
coolant levels should be checked. In cold weather conditions, the Engine Heater 
can be programmed to run continuously and allowed to warm the engine 
overnight. When weather becomes extremely cold (below -30ºC) it may be 
necessary to leave the engine running overnight to prevent freezing. 
 
In summer, or when ambient temperature is above freezing, 15 minutes warming 
time should be sufficient. However, when the ambient temperature drops to 
below freezing, the rig engine should be warmed until the temperature gauge in 
the cab reaches the normal operating temperature range. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
FIGURE 123: TYPICAL LOCATION OF THE 
ENGINE PRE-HETAER

THE IDLE AT 1000 – 1100 RPM WHILE WARMING. IN COLD WEATHER, 
ALWAYS ENGAGE THE HYDRAULICS AT A LOW RPM AND GRADUALLY 
INCREASE AS THE SYSTEM WARMS. RUNNIGN THE HYDRAULICS AT 
NORMAL SPEEDS WHEN COLD CAN DAMAGE COMPONENTS.

5.2.2 Hydraulics Warming Guidelines (cold weather only) 
In addition to warming the engine, the hydraulic system m ust be warmed as well. 
Operating the hydraulic systems without properly warming the hydraulic fluid will 
cause unpredictable behavior from some hydraulic functions and may damage 
some systems.  
 
The engine heater circuit is designed so that once the engine  has re ached 
operating temperature the engine heater can be shut down and the hot engine 
coolant routed through a heating coil in the hydraulic tank. This allows the 
hydraulic fluid to be warmed while transporting the Mobile Gripper to the job  if 
necessary.  
 
Unless the hydraulic pumps are engaged  the engine heater only warms the 
hydraulic fluid in the reservoir. Prior to starting the job, the warmed hydraulic fluid 
must be circulated to the various hydraulic systems on the Mobile Gripper. On 
the Universal MG Deck ensure that the hydraulic cooler fans are not turning.  
 
Once on site, engage the PTO.  
 Prior to spotting the Mobile Gripper on the lease , allow the gripper motors 
to turn continuously using minimal up pressure . This will keep the fluid in 
the hydraulic reservoir circulating and allow the engine to heat the main 
pump. In cold weather, start the PTO in a lower gear and increase as the 
fluid gets warm. 
 Open the safety bleed valve on the operator’s console. This will allow fluid 
to circulate through the safety circuit  and heat the Auxiliary pump.  Make 
sure to close the saf ety bleed before beginning work as this will prevent 
the pump from building pressure. 
 
Warm the fluid on site for as long as necessary for the conditions.  Although fluid 
will not circulate in the crane while warming, once in use, the crane will be 
supplied with warmed oil from the reservoir. The fluid to the outriggers and chain 
tension cylinders will never warm completely. To reduce the stress on the seals 
and outrigger cylinders, do not operate these functions more than necessary in 
very cold conditions.  
CAUTION 
THE ENGINE HEATER CIRCUIT MUST BE MANAGED CLOSELY TO 
MAINTAIN A SAFE WORKING TEMPERATURE FOR THE HYDRAULIC 
SYSTEM. IN COLD WEATHER THE HYDRAULIC FLUID MUST BE KEPT 
AT A MINIMUM TEMPERATURE OF 0ºC. IN WARMER WEATHER, 
EMSURE THAT THE WARMING CIRCUIT IS CLOSE D AND THE HEATER 
IS TURNED OFF TO PREVENT THE HYDRAULIC FLUID FROM HEATING 
ABOVE 70ºC.

5.2.3 Daily Inspections 
The following components of the Mobile Gripper must be inspected and/or 
function tested each day.  
 
Refer to the Universal Mobile Gripper Deck Supplement  for locations of 
these items and their controls. Although items may be located differently, 
their functions are identical on that unit and they must be tested in the 
same manner. 
 
The Positive Air Shut Downs  – The emergency shutdown switches are located 
on the operato r’s console and in the cab of the truck. When activated, a valve 
(the Roda valve) is closed in the engine’s intake system, killing the truck’s 
engine. It must be confirmed each day that the Roda valve does in fact kill the 
truck engine and that each shut off switch does activate the Roda valve. 
 
The Rod Safety Clamp  – The safety must be visually inspected and function 
tested each day. 
 
The B low Out Preventer – The BOP requires a visual inspection and a function 
test each day to ensure that the unit itself has no leaks as well as ensuring that 
the closing system is functioning effectively and able to fully close the BOP.  
 
The Crane – The crane must be visuall y inspected for structural integrity daily. 
Inspect hydraulic lines for leaks, and visually inspect the main lifting hook for 
proper operation.  
 
Overhead Lifting Equipment – The lifting accessories such as the Rod 
Elevators, Dog Collar and Lift Mandrel mu st be inspected visually for defects. In 
addition all shackles, wire rope slings and web slings must be visually inspected. 
 
Rod Guide Sections  – At least once each day the handles and pivot lugs on 
each guide section must be inspected for excessive wear o r indications of 
damage or failure. The guide swivel(s) must also be inspected to ensure proper 
function and good condition. 
 
Mobile COROD Injector  – The injector must be visually inspected each day. 
This inspection should include the condition of the Grip per and Roller chains, the 
condition of the welds on the body of the injector, the condition of the hydraulic 
hoses and other components for leaks and the structural integrity of the lifting 
apparatus and any other lifting or hoisting hook on the unit. 
All daily inspections must be recorded in the Corod Operations Log Book.

5.2.3.1 Positive air shut downs 
There are two emergency shut down switches. One is located on the main 
control panel below the Down Pressure gauge. The other is located in the cab of 
the truck. They consist of an electric switch that closes the Roda Air Solenoid to 
kill the engine, stopping the hydraulics. Activating the emergency shut downs is 
very hard on the truck engine. Only kill the engine this way once and do so from 
a low idle when testing. 
 
1. Start the engine and leave it on a low engine idle speed.  
2. Apply the emergency shut off switch on the operator control panel. The 
truck engine should shut off immediately.  
3. Raise the hood and manually reset the Roda valve. 
4. Without restarting the engine, activate the switch in the cab. Check to 
ensure that the Roda valve was activated. It is not necessary to kill the 
engine more than once.

5.2.3.2 Rod Safety Clamp Accumulator Check 
The rod safety clamp accumulator check is designed to ensure that the 
accumulator pre-charge pressure is sufficient enough to provide the rig’s crew 
time to install a mechanical rod clamp on the rod string before the pressure in the 
safety system depletes to the point at which the safety clamp can no longer hold 
the rod. 
 
The rod safety  clamp on a mobile gripper unit is powered by the unit’s system 
pump, which is regulated to 2,800 psi (1 9.3 MPa). If the safety pressure gauge 
does not register 2,800 psi ± 100 psi (19.3 MPa ± 0.69 MPa), the system must be 
checked by a technician and adjusted as required. 
 
The check is as follows: 
 
1. Ensure the pressure in the safety system has been completely relieved 
2. Engage the hydraulic system 
3. Observe the safety and system pressure gauges 
 The safety pressure gauge will begin rising quickly to a certai n 
pressure. Once that pressure is reached, the needle will rise at a 
slower rate until it reaches 2,800 psi (19.3 MPa) 
 When the system pressure gauge spikes (rises sharply), the 
pressure on the safety pressure gauge will show the pre -charge 
pressure 
 
If the gauge needle speed changes above or below 1,200 psi ± 100 psi (8.3 MPa 
±0.69 MPa), the pre -charge pressure may be incorrectly set and must be 
serviced. 
  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18

5.2.3.3 Safety Inspection 
Visually inspect the Rod Safety.  
 
 Check each part of the body 
for cracks. Pay particular 
attention to the area around 
the threaded rod holes and 
the gripper guide tube. 
 Check the shackles and 
tabs of the lifting eyes for 
cracking and distortion. 
 Inspect the pad a ssembly 
on the rod end of the 
hydraulic cylinder. This is 
the part of the safety which 
is under the most stress 
during operation. 
 Check the hydraulic cylinder 
and fittings for leaks. 
 Look for cracks on the pads or any indication that the pad has been closed 
on an uneven part of the rod. 
 Inspect the thickness of the 
pads. If the pads show 
rounded edges or significant 
reduction of material 
thickness they should be 
replaced. 
 Pads must be aligned so 
that when closed, the pads 
interlock perfectly. If the 
alignment is not precise, the 
safety’s gripping ability will 
be compromised. 
 Ensure that the Safety Plate 
is pinned in place 
 Inspect the two ¾” hydraulic 
hoses and their fittings for 
leaks or any damage.

5.2.3.4 BOP Inspection 
1. Visually inspect the Reagan BOP for loose bolts, cracks or any other 
damage. 
2. Install a piece of rod into the Reagan. The BOP must be able to close on 
the smallest rod in use at the well . Frequent closure of the BOP can 
reduce the life of the rubber element. Don’t close the BOP on itself except 
in emergencies. If the BOP is closed on itself, change the rubber before 
proceeding. 
3. Hook up the BOP hose and pump up the Reagan to 1250psi. If there is no 
gauge, pump until there is significant resist ance to the pump handle. A 
gauge should be installed at the next scheduled service. 
4. Ensure the fluid reservoir still has fluid in it. 
5. Leave pressure on it for  several minutes and check the gauge to ensure 
pressure has not been lost. If there is no gauge, o ne must be installed at 
the next scheduled maintenance. 
6. Test the BOP using all closing systems (Baker pump and Air over 
Hydraulics if equipped) to verify proper function of all systems. 
 
Reagan must be able to be closed within 1 minute  as per EUB regulatio ns. 
Consult your local regulatory body for requirements which may apply. 
 
If the Reagan BOP leaks  or will not hold pressure  it must be removed from 
service immediately.

5.2.3.5 Crane Inspection 
The MG operator is required to carefully visually inspect the crane  prior to work  
each day. Inspect the crane’s base for cracking and ensure that the bolts whic h 
secure the crane to the rig deck are secure. Check the hydraulic hoses and 
cylinders of the crane for leaking or signs of damage. Refer to Section 23 of the 
Weatherford Global Safety Manual for Cranes and Rigging guidelines. 
Check the following: 
 
 Welds on Tie-Down brackets 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Welds on deck brackets 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Condition and tightness  
of Tie-Down bolts

transmitted directly into the truck 
chassis. It is necessary to inspect 
thoroughly all the connections from the 
crane all the way to the chassis ( i.e. the u-
bolts etc.). 
 
The boom arms must be inspected each 
day for cracks or damage to the hard 
lines.  
 
Pay special attention to the storage 
roller on the boom arm as cracks 
tend to form there regularly.

5.2.3.6 Rod Elevators 
 
A daily Level I visual inspection of the overhead lifting equipment must be 
performed. When inspecting the Rod Elevators, begin with the bale. Check the 
inner radius of the bale for excessive wear. Inspect the entire bale for cracking or 
spreading. Inspect the entire body for cracks as well.

moves up/down more than 1/8” replace the bail 
or consult OEM. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The top face of the body is f itted with a 
replaceable insert. The insert is designed to be 
replaced when worn. Replace as necessary 
and ensure that the retaining screws are tight.

5.2.3.7 Wire Ropes and Slings 
 
Every wire rope sling on the Mobile Gripper must be inspected daily for broken 
wires and other types of damage. Refer to Appendix H for wire rope inspection 
information. Nylon web slings must be inspected for cutting or excessive wear 
and removed from service if more than 25% of the width of the sling  
 
Each Sling whether wire rope or nylon web must have a tag which indicates its 
serial number, date of manufacture and rated working load limit. Verify that each 
sling is tagged and that the tag is clearly legible. 
 
Pull test certification documents of each sling must also be maintained and the 
serial number of each sling must be recorded in the Mobile Gripper Operations 
Logbook.

5.2.3.8 Rod Guide Lifting Lugs & Pivot Lugs 
 
Sections #1 and #4 of  the Mobile Gripper’s Rod Guide have lifting lugs attached 
for the Service Rig/Flushby’s auxiliary winch to hoist the guide into position. 
Inspect the lugs visually each day looking for bending or cracking in the welds. 
Check each of the guide sections’ co nnection lugs for spreading or cracking. If 
any defect is found or suspected, the guide must be taken out of service until 
repaired. Inspect the Clevis on the lug for cracking or deformation. For overhead 
loads, the clevis must be closed with a nut and bolt. 
 
The Fassi Crane, the Rod Elevators and the  lifting lugs are considered critical 
overhead lifting devices and must be inspected according to the schedule 
described in the document COROD Level III Overhead Lifting Equipment 
Inspection Manual . According t o the policies outlined in that document, all 
overhead lifting equipment must be visually inspected daily. 
 
5.2.3.9 Mobile Gripper Lifting Apparatus 
 
Every mobile injector has a Slant Lifting Apparatus which is adjustable to allow 
the unit to be lifted at various angles for installation on slant wells. This lifting 
fixture is also usually used when moving the Injector onto or off of  the deck of the 
Mobile Gripper. 
 
 
Inspect the entire Slant Lifting 
Apparatus for cracking, bent 
members, worn bolt holes or any 
other sign of damage or wear.

5.2.3.10 Gripper Chains and Pads 
 
Before beginning any job it is important to assess the condition of the pads on 
the Gripper Chains. In general, each Mobile Gripper will perform similar jobs  with 
similar types of COROD from day to day. As a result the gripper chains will wear 
in a way which is best suited to that type of COROD. If the Mobile Gripper is 
required to perform a job which is unusually deep or using a different COROD 
type or size than usual, the Gripper Chains may not be adequate.  
 
In the case shown in figure 64 , the pads 
are worn due to continued use on large 
elliptical rod. As a result contact with 
small round COROD is very limited and 
the pads may not be able to grip the 
smaller string effectively.  Other 
examples of this type of pad wear issue 
are shown as well.

5.2.3.11 Hydraulic Fluid Level 
 
Each day, prior to beginning work, it is 
necessary to ensure that there is sufficient 
hydraulic fluid in the hydraulic reservoir to safely 
operate the functions of the hydraulic system. 
The volume of hydraulic fluid in the reservoir is 
calculated to ensure that sufficient cooling can 
take place. Operating the Mobile Gripper’s 
hydraulic system with insufficient hydraulic fluid 
can cause elevated temperatures in the 
hydraulic system , ultimately damaging 
components.  
 
Hydraulic fluid level should be cold when 
checked. The sight glass is located on the 
passenger side of the tank facing the rear of the 
vehicle. Use only new Univis N32 Hydraulic oil 
from a clean container.

5.3 POSITIONING THE MG ON THE WELL 
 
Because the MG must always work in conjunction with at least one  piece, and 
sometimes two other pieces of service equipment, positioning is very important. 
The MG must be located in such a way that  it can support the guide if necessary 
and is close enough for the tethered hydraulic hoses to reach the injector unit. 
 
Typically, the service rig or flushby assisting the MG will be positioned on the well 
first. As the MG operator, try to be involved in the planning of the service rig’s 
position. Ensure that sufficient room is left for the MG to be positioned on firm, 
level ground in the proper orientation. Consult with the Service Rig crew to 
ensure that the service reel will be placed where it won’t int erfere with their 
operations. 
 
There are two most common, but many different  orientations of the MG and 
service reel based on the number and position of wells on the lease  and what 
other equipment is required for the j ob. If there is only one well, or if w ells are 
spaced at a large enough distance, it is common for the service rig and MG to be 
90º apart with the service reel between the two as shown in the diagram. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
FIGURE 146: COMMON EQUIPMENT POSITIONS

In cases where wells are too closely spaced for the MG to spot off the side, the 
MG can spot parallel to the ser vice rig at a distance of about 2 meters. This is 
shown in the next diagram.  The main concern is that the picker can safely pass 
the injector to the service rig. The rig’s line must be within 15 feet or so of the 
picker base. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
In any case, be sure to position the MG so that the crane is within easy reach of 
the work area. Keep in mind that the lifting capacity of the Fassi Crane decreases 
as the boom is extended. With the weight of the Injector at approximately 
4500lbs, even the small est crane currently in service is safe to an extended 
length of 15 feet when parallel to the ground. The crane should not be extended 
more than 15 feet while lifting the injector unit. It is important to determine the 
position and spacing of the service eq uipment, including the service reel, guide 
rack etc., during the pre-job meeting before spotting in the work area.

Always use a spotter when backing the MG up to  the well. Enlist the assistance 
of a member of the Service R ig/Flushby crew . Before spotting the Mobile 
Gripper, it is important to observe the lease and note any hazards or 
obstructions. The spotter should always be in clear view of the driver and have 
full view of the hazard being avoided. Use clear hand signals (Section 4.14.1) to 
direct the vehicle around the obstruction. Once the Mobile Gripper has been 
maneuvered passed a particular obstruction, walk around the vehicle again to 
reassess the r oute. Continue directing the vehicle and reassessing the route as 
necessary until the unit is in position. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The service reel must be at a distance from the well which allows the Rod Guide 
to reach it. This distance may change as a result of the accessories used to 
guide the COROD, but in general the maximum reach of the Rod Guide is 30 – 
35 feet from well center. 
 
Some method of securing the Rod Guide must be available. The Service Reel 
Rod Guide is always available, but if it cannot be used, the Fassi Crane must be 
able to reach the Rod Guide mouth to support it. The Crane’s maximum reach is 
approximately 40 feet. 
 
Position the Mobile Gripper in such a way as to allow these conditions to be met.

5.4 ENGAGING THE HYDRAULIC PUMP DRIVE SYSTEM 
 
In order to safely engage the hydraulic pump drive, perform the following steps 
with the rig engine warm and running at idle . Make sure to allow time for each 
step to be positively completed before engaging the next step. 
 
1. Depress the clutch pedal and hold for 5 seconds. 
2. After waiting for at least 5 seconds, m ove the hydraulic drive selector 
switch from “Road” to “Hydraulics”. Allow a few seconds for the pneumatic 
valve to move the actuator. 
3. Select the appropriate gear according to the label on the dash of the MG. 
4. Release the clutch pedal  slowly to allow the gears in the transfer case to 
mesh if they have not on their own . Allow ti me for the transmission to 
engage.  
5. Use the truck’s cruise control to select the correct engine speed. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Although the engagement process is similar, r efer to the Universal Mobile 
Gripper Deck supplement  for description of the pneumatic switch used in 
those units.

5.6 SETTING THE OUTRIGGERS 
 
Although the loads lifted by the MG’s picker crane are not of the magnitude of a 
Corig, the range of the picker arm creates the need for additional stability.  It is 
important to the safe operation of the mobile gripper that the outriggers be used 
to stabilize the unit before lifting items off the deck.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
It is critical to the support of the u nit to fully extend the outriggers and use the 
wooden pads to achieve a firm, stable footing. The unit should be leveled before 
anything is lifted with the knuckle picker.  With the outriggers in place and the

5.7 INSTALLING THE ROD WIPER SAFETY AND BOP 
 
Among the first items to be unloaded from the deck is the Rod Wiper , Safety and 
BOP Stack. It is important to get the safety in position on the COROD as soon as 
possible. On a job where th e pump rotor is to be surfaced , the service rig crew 
will have already removed the drive unit and installed a six foot pony rod with a 
bullet. Ensure that the Rod Wiper caps are loose and the safety is open and 
lower the stack over the exposed end of the rod string.  
 
On a Rerun or Install, the well will be empty and capped prior to starting t he job. 
The service rig crew will confirm that the well is dead and remove the cap. The 
BOP stack is installed first on these types of jobs. The Pump rotor is then run 
through the open stack. Once the pump rotor is lowered into the well, the service 
rig crew will install the shear coupling and support the pump rotor and shear on a 
metal plate over the Rod Safety using a rod elevator.

The orientation of th e BOP stack is important to the final position of the rod 
guide. The guide should be positioned so that the COROD is guided off the side 
of the injector unit. This ensures that the curvature of the rod presses against the 
gripper chain keeping it centered in the chain. This will affect the orientation of 
the BOP stack when working with elliptical COROD.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
When working with elliptical COROD the Injector and Safety must be oriented 
this way to allow both units to grip the COROD on the major axis. When working 
with round COROD (shown on the next page) the Safety can be turned 90º which 
allows the Safety/Injector Guide tube to be used to align the Injector and Safety.

safely, remove the Extend side hose with the Safety off and remove the Retract 
side hose with the Safety applied.  This method should also be used when 
reinstalling the hoses as a pressurized hose will be very difficult to install.

5.8 UNLOADING THE GUIDE RACK  
 
Unload the Guide Rack and Service Reel before installing the pulling unit so that 
while the pulling unit is being positioned, others can work on assembling the reel 
and the Rod Guide can be assembled as soon as the pulling unit is in place. 
 
The Rod Guide is assembled by hand. Although the sections are small, it is 
helpful to place the guide rack reasonably close to the work area. Guide sections 
are stored on both sides of the rack, so place it in a position which al lows access 
all around the rack.  
Remove the retaining rods before 
attempting to lift the guide rack. Always 
enlist the assistance of another worker to 
steady the rack as it is being lowered into  
place. Ensure that the rack is firmly panted 
and steadily in position. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
INSPECT THE ENTIRE GUIDE RACK INCLUDING THE LIFTING LUGS, 
SLINGS AND SHACKLES BEFORE LIFTING IT OFF THE DECK  OF THE 
MOBILE GRIPPER.

5.9 WORKING WITH REELS 
Whenever a length COROD Continuous Rod must be transported or stored a 
Reel is used.  
 
When rod is being transported to a customer for a ne w installation it is stored and 
moved on a Transport Reel. This type of reel is designed to work in conjunction 
with a Transport Trailer and is also often used as a long term storage system for 
new COROD.  
 
During service work, if a string needs to be remo ved from a well for any reason, 
the rod is placed on a Service Reel. The service reel is a  temporary, collapsible 
storage system for the COROD. Its collapsible design allows each Mobile 
Gripper and Corig™ to carry a service reel with them from job to job. 
 
There are variations of each type of COROD reel and each one serves a specific 
purpose.

5.9.1 Service Reels 
Service reels are designed to be fully collapsible to allow for ease of use and 
transportation. Each Slant rig can carry one collapsed Service Reel on the 
passenger’s side on top of the rear fender.  
 
There are three main variations of the standard Service  Reel. The Single Pocket 
Service Reel is the type most commonly used in field service operations and is 
the simplest to assemble and use. When assembled it has a single containment 
area, called the “Pocket”, into which the COROD is fed from the Rod Guide.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The Multi Pocket Service Reel can accommodate several strings of COROD on 
one reel. This is useful when performing multipl e jobs in fields with multiple wells 
close together. Multi Pocket Service Reels can have 2 or 4 pockets. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The Multi Pocket Service Reel is typically used in fields where wells are close 
together and the reel can easily be moved from well to well on the same pad for 
multiple jobs.

The adjustable reel is used when working with COROD that has been in storage. 
In order to remove COROD from a service reel for storage it must be tightly 
banded together. Over time spools of COROD may change shape and no longer 
fit into a conventional single pocket service reel. The arms of the adjustable reel 
can move to accommodate any stretching or settling that has occurred in the reel 
during storage.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The maximum length of COROD string which can be safely loaded onto any 
service reel is a function of the string’s size and linear weight. Refer to section 
3.2 in the General Information for weights and nominal sizes to ensure that your 
service reel is not overloaded.  
 
The heavy duty service reel is a single pocket service reel with a higher load 
capacity than the standard service reel. Table 8 shows the capacities for the 
single pocket and heavy duty service reels and lists their specific drawing 
numbers.

5.9.2 Unloading the Service Reel 
 
The service reel must be positioned so that the mouth of the rod guide is situated 
over the pocket of the reel. The reel can be moved if necessary a fter it is 
assembled. It should be placed roughly 10 meters from the pulling unit at a 
position which will allow the guide to clear  any obstructions. It may be necessary 
to use the crane to stabilize the guide if there is no room for the service reel rod 
guide. Keep this in mind when positioning the reel. 
 
INSPECT THE SERVICE REEL AND ITS LIFTING CHAINS AND SHACKLES 
BEFORE LIFTING IT OFF THE DECK OF THE MOBILE GRIPPER.

Regardless of the position of other equipment on site, the mouth of the guide 
must be approximately in line with the center line of the service reel. Depending 
on the position and orientation of the rod guide, the guide mouth may be as much 
as 33 feet from the well. The mouth of the guide may be turned somewhat inward 
to adjust for slightly closer spacing. Place the service reel in a location whic h will 
not interfere with the service rig’s operations. 
 
When performing a Surface, the full service reel will usually be left on site. Make 
sure to place it in a position where it will not obstruct the service crew performing 
work on the well prior to the Rerun.

base as you are assembling the reel.

remaining 2 legs.

5.9.4 Service Reel Rod Guide Support 
 
The Service Reel Rod G uide is 
designed to secure the mouth of the 
rod guide to the service reel.  This is 
the preferred method of  securing the 
guide mouth, howe ver alternative 
means may be used if necessary.

from the base of the service reel and 
replace it with the service reel rod 
guide base.  It is important to secure 
the base in the service reel with a 
length of chain to prevent it from being 
pulled out by the rod guide.  
 
Install the Service Reel Rod Guid e 
Bracket. 
 
Adjust the height and length of the 
service reel rod guide to bring it to the 
desired position and secure the rod 
guide mouth to it with the supplied pin.

5.10 INSTALLING THE PULLING UNIT 
 
The pulling unit is by far the largest and heaviest  individual item on the Mobile 
Gripper’s deck. Exercise extreme caution when lifting the pulling unit. Do not lift 
the pulling unit witho ut extending the outriggers and leveling the Mobile Gripper 
unit. 
 
INSPECT THE INJECTOR’S LIFTING APPARATUS, LIFTING LUGS  AND ITS 
LIFTING CHAINS AND SHACKLES BEFORE LIFTING IT OFF THE DECK OF 
THE MOBILE GRIPPER. 
5.10.1 Lifting the Mobile Injector 
The pulling unit is pinned to the deck 
at the feet. These pins need to be 
removed before lifting the pulling unit. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The Mobile COROD Injector is lifted off the deck using the rigid lifting apparatus. 
This apparatus is adjustable to allow the Injector to be lifted into position at a 
variety of angles corresponding to the angle of the mast of the unit which will 
support it during the job.

5.10.2 The Three Part Lifting Sling and Weight Adjusting Arm 
 
The three part sling and weight adjusting lifting 
arm are connected to the same lug system as 
the traditional sling, however the weight 
adjusting arm alleviates the need to relocate 
the shackles to plumb the Injector. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The weight adjusting arm has a threaded rod 
which passes through a spring and threads into 
a square spring seat with  a retaining not at the 
bottom of the assembly and an adjusting nut at 
the top of the assembly. 
 
 
 
 
The starting length of the arm assembly is 
adjusted by loosening the top and bottom nuts 
and unthreading the rod from the spring seat.  
 
The tension on the spring is adjusted by 
tightening or loosening the top nut. This spring 
preload adjusts the weight required to begin 
compressing the spring once the Injector is in 
the air.

AND WEIGHT ADJUSTING ARM

When surfacing a pump, the service rig crew will have raised the rod string after 
the BOP stack was installed. The COROD will have to be aligned  in the gripper 
chains before t he Safety  Injector Guide Tube can be aligned with the safety’s 
receiver tube. To align the rod in the Injector it must be raised to a height of 
approximately seven feet above the Safety Clamp.  In the case of a rerun or new 
install, the service rig crew will have already lowered the pump rotor through the 
BOP Stack  into the well and the COROD will be aligned in the gripper chains 
after running it through the rod guide. 
 
Ensure that the pressure beams are in 
the open position and carefully guide 
the injector over the COROD. Position 
the injector over the safety and align 
the safety/injector guide tube with the 
receiver tube on the safety. Lower the 
pulling unit until the guide tu be is 
engaged in the safety.  
 
 
 
Align the rod so  that it is centered in the 
gripper pads of the gripper chain and 
lightly close the pressure beam on the 
rod to hold it in this posi tion on the rod. 
Do not close the pressure beam until 
the COROD is aligned in the chains or 
damage to the chains may occur.

5.11 ROD GUIDE ASSEMBLY 
 
 
 
 
 
Similar to the Corig, the Mobile Gripper guide is assembled from multiple 
sections. The seventh section has a swivel to allow the guide mouth to be 
oriented toward the service reel regardless of it’s location in relat ion to the well. 
When the Mobile Gripper is used in slant applications, an alternate second 
section with an additional swivel can be used t o allow the guide to be in a vertical 
position when the gripper is not.

be assembled in the ideal manner.

5.12 USING THE MOBILE GRIPPER IN SLANT POSITION 
 
Occasionally the Mobile Gripper will have to be used to perform work on slant 
wells. Although the jobs themselves are not substantially different when 
performed in slant mode, the Rod Guide must be rigged up  in a modified fashion 
and special preparation of the Injector is required to work with the slant  mast of 
the Service Rig.  
 
The majority of the information in section 4 applies to the slant operation of the 
Mobile Gripper. However several differences must  be noted. Each point is 
discussed in the following sections. 
 
It is important to recognize that the nature of the slant orientation  introduces an 
element of added difficulty to many of the tasks which would typically be 
considered straight forward. Liftin g and engaging equipment at angles  requires 
an extreme attention to detail to reduce hazards to both equipment and workers.  
This adds to the level of detail necessary in  the Pre Job Hazard Assessment and 
Pre Job Meeting. During this meeting pay particular attention to the lifting of non -
vertical loads, working in unusually close quarters, workers avoiding walking or 
working below overhead loads and pinch points between objects being installed 
inside the mast of the Service Rig. 
 
It is important to note that this type of job cannot be done using a Flushby. The 
Flushby does not have the necessary adjustability or manpower to properly 
perform the job.  
 
MOBILE GRIPPER UNITS MUST BE EQUIPPED WITH FASSI F150A.24 
MODEL CRANE OR E QUIVALENT AT A MINUMUM TO PERFORM SLANT 
JOBS. 
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18

5.12.1 Positioning the MG on the Well 
 
When in the slant position the Service Rig’s auxiliary winch is not overhead and 
cannot be used  to hoist the Rod Guide to its working height. As a result the 
Mobile Gripper’s Knuckle Picker must be used to elevate the Rod Guide. In order 
to be in position to do so, The MG must be spotted closer to the Service Rig than 
it would for a vertical job.  
 
In addition b ecause the Rig’s mast is not vertical, the Rod Guide must be 
oriented to one side or the other. As a result, there are only two spotting positions 
which allow the MG’s knuckle picker to reach the top of the Rod Guide.

5.12.2 Installing the BOP Stack 
 
There are two important considerations when installing the BOP Stack in the 
slant position. The object must be lifted and placed onto the well at the same 
angle as the  well in order for it to be easily  mated to the well.  To achieve this 
angled lift, a load leveler and angle finder are used.

angle of the load.

to match the angle of the BOP stack to  
the angle of the well.
