APPENDIX J – GLOSSARY OF TERMS 
 
Blank or Blind Bullet: A bullet made from a piece of tubing closed on one 
end and secured on the end of a parted COROD 
string to prevent the end from damaging the rod 
guide. 
 
Blow Out Preventer (BOP) A device used for controlling the pressure in an oil 
well in order to prevent the well fluid or gases from 
escaping while the well is being serviced. 
 
 
BOP Plate: A steel plate which covers the BOP to allow the 
COROD string to be securely suspended using a Rod 
Clamp or Rod Elevator. 
 
Corod BOP: A type of Blow Out Preventer designed by COROD 
which uses hydraulic pressure to compress a two 
piece solid rubber element against the rod in the well, 
sealing the pressure from blowing out. 
 
COROD: A continuous for m of sucker rod with no couplings 
available in round and semi -elliptical shapes and 
multiple sizes and grades for various applications. 
 
Crimp: A defect in the well tubing caused by improper 
handling. May adversely affe ct the installation of the 
down-hole pump. 
 
Crown Out: The point at which  the traveling blocks rise to make 
contact with the crown of the mast. If not prevented, 
serious damage to the mast can occur. 
 
Dog Collar: A type of clamp consisting of multiple articulating 
sections, each containing a toothed pad similar to a 
fishing sli p. Its segmented design allows it to clamp 
irregularly shaped items such as tubing. 
 
Down Pressure: The operating pressure in the main hydraulic system 
which controls the force with which the Grip per 
Motors are operated in the down direction. 
 
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18 

===== PAGE 325 =====
 
 
COROD® 
Continuous R od & Well Services 
 
© 2015 Weatherford International, Inc. 
This document is copy righted and, whether patentable or non -patentable subject matter, embodies the proprietary  and conf idential inf ormation of  
Weatherf ord. Recipients agree it is loaned in conf idence with the understanding that neither it , nor the inf ormation in it, will be reproduced, used or di sclos ed ,  
in whole nor in part f or any  purpose except the limited purpose f or which it is loaned. This document shall be returned to We atherf ord upon de mand. 
 
325 
 
Fishing: The act of retrieving the bottom section of a parted 
COROD string. The upper portion of the parted string 
is removed from the well and a special “Fishing Tool” 
is welded to the free end. This tool is sent back down 
into the well to “Fish” the bottom section o f the parted 
string. 
 
Fishing Tool: A tool assembly designed to slide over the broken 
end of a parted rod string and secure it with a  spring 
loaded, toothed gripping mechanism called a “slip”. 
 
Flow Tee: A high pressure fitting used at the wellhead just above 
the Bonnet (wellhead cap) to allow the well fluid to be 
diverted from the production tubing to the flow line or 
other parts of the well as required. 
 
Gripper Chains: A chain constructed of #120 links, Gripper Housings 
and Gripper Pads which are driven by the sprockets 
of the Injector in a Mobile Gripper or Corig to move 
COROD into or out of a well. 
 
Gripper Motors: The hydraulic motors on the Injector Unit which drive 
the Gripper Chains. 
 
Gripper Pad: The aluminum pads of the Gripper Chains that 
actually contact the COROD. 
 
Guide Rack: The storage rack which holds the section of the Rod 
Guide when the guide is not assembled. The Guide 
Rack is secured to the deck of the Mobile Gripper with 
locking pins for transport. 
 
Injector: The purposely designed hydraulic machine used to 
move COROD into or out of a well. 
 
 
 
Insert Type Pump: A reciprocating down hole pump. The major 
advantage of this type of pump is that the barrel and 
plunger are a single unit, simp lifying service of the 
pump.

Jarring: Applying a specific tensile load to the COROD string 
to stretch the rod and then releasing the tension 
quickly, but only a short distance. The quick 
contraction of the stretched COROD causes the 
plunger of the recipro cating pump to hammer against 
the upper stop, unseating the pump more effectively 
than a straight pull. 
 
Kick: An undesired influx of reservoir fluid into the well bore 
which can force well fluid or gas to the surface at a 
velocity that may be hazardous. 
 
 
Killing the well: Any of various methods used to stop an active well 
from flowing. 
 
Lift Mandrel: A lifting device designed to secure the D -Ring of the 
Injector’s lifting sling and be lifted by the Service Rig’s 
Tubing Elevator. This makes it possible to lift the 
Injector without rigging out the Service Rig’s Tubing 
Elevator.

Equipment (PPE): The safety apparatus and clothing items which each 
Weatherford employee is required to wear when 
working on lease . These typically include, but a re not 
limited to eye protection, hard hat, gloves, fire 
retardant coveralls and steel toed boots. 
 
Pick up Sub/Pony Rod: A short sucker rod in 2, 4 and 6 foot lengths used for 
lifting the COROD string to perform various service 
tasks. 
 
Polish Rod Wheel: A custom made tool designed to clamp onto the 
COROD string which allows crew members to 
manually rotate the string in the well in order to assist 
with the latching of fishing tools. 
Positive Air Shutdowns: A switch or switches  installed on all COROD mobile 
equipment to allow the operator to immediately stop 
the truck engine in the case of an emergency. The 
switch closes a valve in the engine’s air intake to 
choke out the engine.

APPENDIX K – WIRE ROPE INSPECTION 
 
See Section 23.12 of the Weatherford Global Safety Manual for more information 
regarding wire rope inspection. 
 
The core of the cable can be insp ected using a Marlin Spike to spread the 
strands. Drive the Marlin spike into the 
core of the wire and use it to spread the 
strands. If there is rust in the core of the 
wire it should be replaced.  
 
 
 
 
Broken Wires 
One broken wire, or a few broken wires widely spaced are not a problem, 
however regular breaks indicate an area of concern. Replace rope if it has 6 
broken wires in one lay or 3 broken wires in one strand. 
 
Broken wires at the socket of the eyelet ca n often be fixed by replacing the eyelet 
and/or socket. However, in this case we will replace the wire rope entirely.

Abrasion 
As the rope passes over drums and 
sheaves it will wear. In general, a wire 
rope should be replaced if its outer 
diameter has d ecreased by 1/3 of the 
original diameter.  Frequently ropes are 
subjected to abnormal scuffing and 
scraping, such as contact with cross 
members of a boom. Look for bright 
spots. Impending internal breakdown should always be suspected when a 
sudden or significant diameter reduction is noted. 
 
 
 
 
This figure shows the correct method of 
measurement of the rope’s diameter. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Stretch 
One rope lay is the length along the rope 
which a single strand requires to make one 
complete spiral, or turn, around the core. 
Over the life of the rope it will naturally 
elongate, changing the length of the lay. As 
a rule, if lengthening of lay is noted, with loss 
of rope diameter, internal break -up or core 
destruction should be suspected.  Another 
common cause of unlaying i s worn sheaves. 
When the bottom of a sheave groove wears, 
it can restrict normal movement as the rope 
enters and leaves the groove. The result can 
be a build -up of twist which can change the 
length of lay.

Crushed Strands 
When too many layers of rope are  wound 
on a drum, it may cause individual strands 
of the rope to be crushed. In general, no 
more than 2 layers of wire rope should be 
wound on a drum, especially with small 
wires such as 6x37. If more layers are 
required a thicker wire rope should be used 
such as 6x19 which has fewer, thicker 
strands. The main winch rope should be 
checked for this condition. COROD rigs 
use 6x36 wire rope in varying thicknesses. 
 
Any condition in which the ropes diameter 
is decreased by 1/3 of its original diameter 
at any point on the rope should result in the 
rope’s replacement. 
 
 
 
Any permanent deformation of the wire 
rope whether from a kink or sudden 
unloading should result in the rope’s 
replacement.  
 
 
 
 
Other common wear types are: Bird -
caging, caused by a sudden unl oading 
causing the strands of the wire to 
permanently spread;  Localized 
Abrasion, which indicates some 
obstruction to the wire rope’s free 
movement; Bending Fatigue, which is 
caused by bending a rope over too small 
a sheave or a badly worn sheave.

APPENDIX M – GENERAL OILFIELD REFERENCE 
ARTIFICIAL LIFT PUMPS  
 
In one form or another COROD is always in the process of working with 
downhole pumps. The most common forms of downhole pumps used with to 
create Artificial Lift are the Reciprocating Rod Pump (RRP) and the Progressing 
Cavity Pump (PCP).

Reciprocating Rod Pumps (RRP) 
 
When the pressure in an oil reservoir is depleted to the point that the wells will no 
longer flow, some means of artificial lift must be used to bring the fluids to the 
surface. The most generally accepted means of artificial  lift is the subsurface 
pump, actuated by a pumping unit and a sucker -rod string. There are two basic 
types of subsurface pumps: the tubing pump and the insert rod pump. 
 
The tubing pump , barrel and seating nipple are threaded directly onto the 
production tubing and installed near the bottom of the well, immersed in the fluids 
within the well casing. The plunger is run into the tubing on the sucker rod or 
COROD string. Usually a standing valve puller is run between the bottom of the 
plunger and the standing valve. This configuration makes removal of the standing 
valve optional when the sucker rods or COROD and plunger are pulled. The 
plunger, traveling valve, and standing valve can be serviced simply by pulling the 
rods; but, to service the pump barrel, the tubing must be removed.  
 
The insert rod pump is run into the well, on the sucker rods  or COROD, as a 
complete unit. A seating assembly on the pump engages into a seating nipple on 
the production tubing when the pump is run in the well. The entire rod pump  is 
removed, and serviced by pulling the sucker rod or COROD string. 
 
The RRP is the type of pump most associated with an articulating pumping unit 
or more widely known as a pump jack. It can also be operated with a piston style 
pumping unit known as a Rotaflex.  
 
The following are examples of standard rod pumps that a typical Corig™ crew 
may encounter 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
Traveling-Barrel, Bottom -Anchor Pumps 
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18 

===== PAGE 336 =====
 
 
COROD® 
Continuous R od & Well Services 
 
© 2015 Weatherford International, Inc. 
This document is copy righted and, whether patentable or non -patentable subject matter, embodies the proprietary  and conf idential inf ormation of  
Weatherf ord. Recipients agree it is loaned in conf idence with the understanding that neither it , nor the inf ormation in it, will be reproduced, used or di sclos ed ,  
in whole nor in part f or any  purpose except the limited purpose f or which it is loaned. This document shall be returned to We atherf ord upon de mand. 
 
336 
 
Traveling-barrel, bottom -anchor pumps are typically used in sandy 
applications and offer the following advantages:  
The movement of the traveling barrel keeps the fluid in motion and sand 
washed clear, almost down to the pump seating nipple. This advantage 
minimizes the possibility of sand settling around the pump and seizing it 
in the tubing. 
Traveling-barrel pumps are particularly suitable for wells that are 
pumped intermittently. Sand cannot settle inside the pump because the 
traveling valve at the top of this pump will close when the well is shut 
down. This prevents sand from settling inside the pump, wh ich could 
cause the plunger to stick or prevent the standing valve from opening 
when the well resumes pumping. 
Both the traveling and standing valves on traveling -barrel pumps have 
open-type cages, which are more rugged in construction and provide 
more fluid passage than closed-type cages. 
The externally and internally equalized pressure on the barrel gives 
bottom-anchor pumps (with either traveling or stationary barrel) greater 
resistance to bursting than top-anchor pumps have. 
Traveling-barrel, bottom -anchor pumps have the following limitations: 
Traveling-barrel pumps are at a disadvantage in wells with a low static 
fluid level. To enter the pumping chamber, the fluid must rise through 
the pull tube and plunger and then pass through the standing valve. 
The standing valve is located on top of the plunger and is smaller in 
diameter than the travel valve and therefore offers more fluid restriction 
than the larger standing valve of stationary -barrel pumps. This promotes 
gas breakout and could cause gas interference in the pump. 
Longer lengths of traveling -barrel pumps should not be run in deep 
wells. When the standing valve (in the plunger top cage) is closed, the 
hydrostatic column load is transmitted by the plunger through the pull 
tube and hold  down, into the pump seating nipple. In a deep well, this 
load will be sufficient to put a bow in a long pull tube, thus setting up a 
drag between the pull plug and the pull tube. 
Traveling-barrel pumps are not usually run in slant holes or in  wells that 
are deviated at the pump seating nipple. Either condition will cause 
excessive wear on the pump barrel and will detract from the travel —and 
therefore reduce the displacement—of the pump. 
 
 
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18 

===== PAGE 337 =====
 
 
COROD® 
Continuous R od & Well Services

Stationary-Barrel, Bottom-Anchor Pumps 
 
Stationary-barrel, bottom -anchor pumps offer the following 
advantages: 
Stationary-barrel, bottom -anchor pumps are ideal for running in 
wells with low static fluid level because the production tubing can 
be run in with only a short perforated nipple or mud anchor below 
the seating nipple. Therefore, if required, the standing valve of the 
pump may be less than 2 ft from the bottom of the well. 
 
Stationary-barrel, bottom -anchor pumps are superior to traveling -
barrel, bottom -anchor pumps for low -fluid-level wells because the  
fluid has only to pass through the larger standing valve, located 
immediately above the pump seating nipple. 
 
Stationary-barrel, bottom -anchor pumps are good for gassy wells 
when the Weatherford full -flow high-compression standing cage is 
used in conjunction with good gas separators or gas anchors. The 
short rise and minimum pressure drop, (as the fluid to passes 
through the standing valve to enter the pump), minimizes the 
tendency to foam and improves pump efficiency. 
 
Bottom-anchored pumps are recommende d for deep wells or wells 
that may pound fluid.  
 
Stationary-barrel, bottom -anchor pumps have the following 
limitations: 
Running a stationary-barrel, bottom-anchor pump in a sandy well is 
hazardous because sand can settle tightly in the annulus between 
the pump and the tubing and make the pump stick tightly in the 
joint  
If the pump is in intermittent -operation mode and the well shuts 
down, sand or other foreign material can settle past the barrel rod 
guide and on top of the pump plunger, possibly causing t he pump to stick when 
the well is put back on production. 
 
 
 
 
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18 

===== PAGE 338 =====
 
 
COROD® 
Continuous R od & Well Services 
 
© 2015 Weatherford International, Inc.

Stationary-Barrel, Top-Anchor Pumps 
Stationary-barrel, top-anchor pumps offer the following advantages: 
Top-anchor pumps are recommended for sandy wells, where bottom -
anchor pumps may occasionally become sanded in and require that 
the tubing and pump be pulled out of the well. The amount of sand that 
can settle over the seating ring or top cup is limited to about 3 in. (76 
mm) because the fluid discharge from the valve rod keeps sand 
washed free above these points. In this respect, top -anchor pumps are 
even superior to traveling-barrel, bottom -anchor pumps.  
If a traveling -barrel pump is spaced too high, sand  can settle around 
the pull tube and up to the lowest point reached by the pull plug on the 
down stroke. 
 
Top-anchor pumps are also recommended in low -fluid-level gassy or 
foamy wells, where submersion of the standing valve in the fluid being 
pumped is particularly advantageous. 
 
Top-anchor pumps should be run in wells that may periodically require 
a pressure or temperature bomb or a measuring device run through 
the seating nipple. The top -lock seating nipple gives almost the full 
opening of the tubing and provides ample clearance for the bomb to 
pass. 
 
The primary limitation of top-anchor pumps is the possibility of a burst 
barrel caused by hydrostatic forces exerted internally on the pump 
barrel. Well depth and the possibility of fluid pound should be care fully 
considered before running a top -anchored pump with a thin-wall barrel. 
If the depth of the well is within the depth recommendations and fluid 
pound is not a problem, a top -anchor pump is a good general-purpose 
installation.  
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18

Progressing Cavity Pumps (PCP) 
 
Progressing Cavity (PC) Pumps are a widely accepted means of artificial lift; with 
high production, lift capacity, and system  efficiency being a few of the major 
benefits over other oil and water pumping systems. The ability for PC pumps to 
handle sand and viscous fluids make them the best solution for heavy oil 
production and the high gas oil ratio tolerance is beneficial  in pumping mixtures 
of sand, oil, gas and water. 
 
PC pumps are comprised of two parts - a helical shaped steel rotor and a stator. 
The rotor, which is the rotating internal  component of the pump, is normally 
driven by a rod string. It is precision machined from high strength steel and 
coated with a wear resistance layer, such as chrome. The stator is attached to 
the produ ction tubing string and remains stationary during  normal operation. It 
consists of a molded elastomer permanently bonded to the inside of a steel tube. 
 
As the PC pump operates, cavities formed between the stator and rotor move 
fluid upward in a non -pulsating action from the  intake to the discharge of the 
pump. Pressure builds linearly from the inlet to the discharge of the pump 
providing the necessary lift to produce fluid to surface.

Pumping Unit Energy Isolation 
There are three main areas of concern with respect to energy isolation on 
reciprocating pumping units: 
 
1. Electrical Power 
2. Mechanical Energy of the Counterweights/Position of Walking Beam 
3. Fluid Pressure 
 
Each of these energy sources can have multiple orientations and configurations 
in the field and can be dependant upon several variables such as: 
 
 Manufacturer 
 Maintenance condition 
 Regional Standards/Requirements 
 Lease conditions 
 
In addition to these functional variables there are some operational variables: 
 
1. In many cases the lease owner has strict internal regulations 
limiting activities which they consider unsafe. 
2. In other cases, the lease owner insists on performing some or all of 
the lockout procedure  and horse head removal themselves. This 
may create difficulties or even preclude WFT workers from 
verifying that certain lockout procedures have been performed

several steps must be taken to ensure safe energy lockout, however each step

o Electrical Fuse Panel

o Are there Customer guidelines to be followed? 
o Is unit equipped with necessary lockout devices?

line of fire/danger zones? 
o Has lockout already been performed? Can it be 
confirmed? 
 
It is not possible to generate a task oriented universal procedure which will allow 
all pumping units to be safely locked out. It is therefore most us eful to suggest 
the following: 
Always perform a Job Safety Analysis. Identify and understand the factors which 
determine the safest lockout method. Develop a plan to lock out the pumping unit 
based on the equipment in use. Ensure that each member of the te am has the 
necessary support and training to safely perform the necessary tasks.

The Weatherford Ampscot  pumping unit uses a motor to drive a set of cranks 
which in turn creates an up and down motion.  This motion is what enables the 
RRP to bring fluid up the wellhead and into the flow line.

There are many different sizes and styles for the conventional pum ping unit 
systems. The sizes are based on load, depth or well and lifting capacity. The 
deeper the well and the more weight the pump has to manage, the larger that 
pumping unit has to be and the longer the pump has to be. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
The styles vary from company to company and region to reg ion. The basic parts 
and functions are the same but there may be differences in the location of the 
components. 
 
One of the primary jobs that a Corig™ has to deal with is connecting or 
disconnecting the polish rod from the pumping unit. The polish rod is c onnected 
to the polish rod hanger which in turn is connected to the horse head. The horse 
head must be removed during operations as it overhangs the well and will 
interfere with the operations of the Corig™ crew. The sizes and styles of the 
horse heads can  vary greatly. The load can be handled with anything from the 
auxiliary winch line, to using the travelling blocks and all the way up to having to 
call in a third party crane company. Before embarking on a job, check with the 
area supervisor to inquire on the size of the horse head.

The Weatherford Rotaflex  pumping unit uses a motor to drive a reversing 
mechanism which in turn moves the load belt and counter weights to create an 
up and down motion.  This motion is what enables the RRP to bring fluid up the 
wellhead and into the flow line. 
 
There are differen t sizes and styles for the Rotaflex pumping unit systems. The 
sizes are based on load, depth or well and lifting capacity. The deeper the well 
and the more weight the pump has to manage, the larger that pumping unit has 
to be.  
 
One of the primary jobs th at a Corig™ has to deal with is connecting or 
disconnecting the polish rod from the pumping unit. The polish rod is connected 
to the polish rod hanger which in turn is connected to the load belt. The Rotaflex 
must be moved during operations as it overhangs  the well and will interfere with 
the operations of the Corig™ crew. The Rotaflex has a sliding base which allows 
the pumping unit to be moved back from the wellhead to allow the Corig™ crew 
access to the well. 
Working with a Rotaflex 
 
This is a general wa lk though for working with a 
Rotaflex for reference only. There are different 
styles and sizes. Before going to a job where this 
needs to be done, the area supervisor needs to fill 
in the Corig™ crew about any special requirements 
that must be addressed. T he area supervisors 
should consult with the original equipment 
manufacturer (OEM) to discuss any special 
installation and assembly issues.  
 
Establish well control. 
 
Stop the unit just as the rod string begins to be lifted 
out of the wellbore. Install two polish rod clamps 
approximately 30 cm (1 ft) below the polish rod 
hanger.  
 
Start unit and rotate through one revolution until the 
string weight is hanging off the polish rod clamps and there is no more load on 
the belt. Apply the brake.

Weatherford’s Rotaflex rollback system is a self -contained, hydraulically powered 
system that moves and repositions the Rotaflex long-stroke pumping unit before 
and after workover operations.  
 
Use of the rollback system is safer, 
easier, and more economical than older 
methods. The system allows one 
operator and an assistant to move a 
Rotaflex pumping unit the specified 
distance of  3m ( 10 ft) in less than 
twenty minutes, whereas other current 
methods require a two - or three-person 
crew with additional equipment. 
 
The rollback system operator must 
maintain positive (touch) control of a 
single lever for the pumping unit to 
move. The system can move the 
pumping unit in 0.5m ( 20”) increments 
up to the maximum of 3m (10 ft).  
Operations stop when lever is released 
to ensure crew safety. 
Alignment marking ensures proper 
ositioning when the Rotaflex unit is 
moved to the operating or rolled-back position. 
 
The method on older units requires rated straps tied to pulling points on the 
Rotaflex unit and a towing vehicle would have to move the unit back from the 
well. 
 
When operations are complete 
on the well, the Rotaflex can be 
moved back into position.  
Reattach the polish rod and 
resume production.

REQUEST TO BE ORIENTED TO 
EQUIPMENT.

The Weatherford Rotary Drive pumping unit for Progressing Cavity Pumps uses 
a motor to rotate the COROD which in turn rotates a helical shaped rotor inside a 
stator at the bottom of the well. The stator is attached to the production tubing 
string and remains stationary during normal operation. As the PC pump operates, 
cavities formed between the rotor and the stator move fluid upward in a non -
pulsating action. This motion is what enables the PCP to bring fluid up the 
wellhead and into the flow line. 
 
There are many different sizes and styles for the rotary drive pumping unit 
systems. The sizes are based on load, depth or well and lifting capacity. The 
deeper the well and the more weight the pump has to manage, the larger that 
pumping unit has to be.  
 
The styles vary from company to company and region to region. The basic parts 
and functions are the same but there may be differences in the location of the 
components. 
 
One of the primary jobs that a Corig™ has to deal with is connecting or 
disconnecting the po lish rod from the pumping unit. The polish rod is connected 
through the stuffing box and drive unit and secured above the drive head with a 
polish rod clamp. The drive unit must be removed during Corig™ operations. The 
sizes of the drive heads can vary gre atly. Before embarking on a job, check with 
the area supervisor to inquire on the size of the drive head. 
 
Working with a PCP Drive Unit 
 
To remove a progressive cavity pump (PCP) drive 
head from operation, ensure that the drive head is 
shut down and all power is locked out prior  to 
beginning any work. The rod string should recoil once 
the motor has been shut down. Once well control is 
established, disconnect the hoses from the hydraulic 
motor or the electrical connections from an electric 
motor. 
Begin by attaching a pony rod and rod coupling to the 
polish rod.  
 
Insert the pony rod into the rod elevator. P ull up on the polished rod to pick up 
the rod weight. Loosen the Polished Rod Support Clamp bolts.  Begin to pull up 
on the polished rod approximately 0.3 m (1 ft). Close the f low line valve to the 
production line. 
  
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18 

===== PAGE 359 =====

Continuous R od & Well Services

Attach the winch line to the lifting lugs on the drive  
head.  Ensure that the drive  head will be balanced.  
Begin to remove the bolts from the wellhead flange 
or unscrew the drive head from the flow tee. 
 
Once the drive has been loosened off, begin to lift 
the drive approximately 0.6 – 1 m (2 –3 ft).  
Install a polish rod clamp above the wellhead and 
below the drive head. Pick up and drop the rod (bump test) three times to ensure 
the rod clamp will hold. 
 
Loosen the stuffing box packing.  
Remove the pony rod from the elevator. Lift 
the drive head off over the polished rod and 
place on the ground beside the well.  Look 
for any damage to polish rod. If there are 
any marks, file them down before 
continuing job.  
 
Ensure there are no cables or hydraulic lines lying underneath the motor that 
could potentially get stuck under the drive head. 
 
Some customers have dedicated storage stands for their motors. Ensure that 
these stands are utilized if provided.

NOTE: The procedure with most modern PCP drive  heads with a retrofit stuffing 
box is to place a polish rod clamp on the stuffing box and then release the polish 
rod from the top. The drive head can be lifted and brought to ground, placed on 
its base with the winch.  This is the safest operation practice, for it allows 
freedom of mobility with the polish rod and keeps the well fully controlled with the 
stuffing box.  . 
 
When installing: 
 
• Record string weight before the rotor enters the stator. 
• Land the rotor on the tagbar. 
• Pull up string weight. 
• Pull up an additional 0.3m (12") above drive head.. 
• Pull up to allow for rod stretch-operating position. 
• Measure and allow for wellhead height-clamping position. 
• Remove sucker rod(s) and replace with the polished rod and pony rods. 
• Run the polish rod into the well and clamp off. 
 
When installing and removing drive 
units using a slant Corig ™ there are 
two types of hoisting styles used. 
 
 
They are known as tagging above 
the drive and tagging below the 
drive. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18 

===== PAGE 361 =====
 
 
COROD® 
Continuous R od & Well Services 
 
© 2015 Weatherford International, Inc.

Tagging above the drive occurs when there are shackles installed on the lifting 
points of the drive unit. Lifting shackles are then installed on the ends of the 
slings and these are used to hoist or lower the drive. 
 
Tagging below the drive refers to strapping the slings below the lifting point on 
the drive unit and hooking both ends of the sling to the hook of the lifting device. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 
PCP Rotor and Drive Head Installation Procedure  (COROD String) 
 
1. Estimate the COROD footage required for the job, this footage can be 
estimated to be a bit less than the pump setting depth of the well.  
2. Connect the rotor to a pony  (6-8ft) 
3. Run the rotor and pony inside the well, on longer pumps (> 20ft) use a 
secondary line (auxiliary winch) connected around the rotor with a nylon 
strap to prevent bending and damage while hoisting the rotor into the 
Derek form the ground. 
4. Connect the COROD shear coupling to the COROD pin end first then to 
the pony rod.

5. Set the COROD counter  
6. Trip the string into the wellbore. 
7. Once the bottom of the rotor reaches a distance approximately close to 
the top of the stator; record the weight of the rod string and/or up pressure 
gauge reading prior to entering the stator and tripping the last few feet of 
rod. Meanwhile compare the footage reading on the counter and compare 
against the setting depth provided in the program. 
8. Continue to run the remaining rod length taking care to lower the string 
very slowly to avoid damaging the stator elastomer. The rod string will 
normally rotate as the rotor enters the stator if sucker rods are used 
however no rotation will be noted with COROD since the rotational 
movement is restricted by the COROD injector grip . , therefore it is  
essential to lower the rotor slowly in order to avoid hitting the tag bar too 
hard. 
9. Continue to lower the rod string until the rotor comes to rest on the tag bar 
at the bottom of the stator. This bottoming out will be indicated by a 
reduction in rod weigh t to near zero. (it helps to have your hands on the 
rod to feel the impact as the rod string contact the tag bar) 
 
Marking on COROD String 
 
10. Once the rotor is resting on the tag bar, Mark the rod at rod BOP level 
(This will be the Zero Weight Mark).  
11. Pull up and out of the stator and lower the rotor again to reconfirm the 
positioning on the tag bar.  
12. With the rotor landed on the tag bar, pull up slowly on the rod string until 
full rod weight and/or up pressure gauge reading (measured in Step 7 
above) is restored. This indicated that the rotor is off the tag bar now.

BOP below the reference point. This is the Operating Point.

24. Cut the COROD string at the Cutting Point mark and weld the Top pin. 
25. Connect the ponies to the COROD top pin 
26. Remove the BOP following the standard procedure already in place. 
27. Attach the polish rod to the top pony rod using a polish rod coupling. To 
ensure proper sealing of the stuffing box, it is critical that the polish rod is 
straight and does not have significant wear or corrosion.

head because of the potential to cause permanent bending of the polish 
rod. DO NOT LEAVE WRENCH MARKS ON THE POLISH ROD. 
28. When properly positioned, run the stuffing box over the polished rod and 
connect it to the top of the CPT (Composite Pumping Tee).

PCP Rotor and Drive Head Installation Procedure (Sucker Rod String) 
 
1. Estimate the number of rods required for the well, this can be calculated 
by dividing the pump setting depth by the length of one sucker rod joint.

2. Connect the rotor to a full -length sucker rod joint (or longest pony) on  the 
ground. 
3. Run the rotor and sucker rod joint inside the well, on longer pumps (> 20ft) 
use a secondary line (auxiliary winch) connected around the rotor with a 
nylon strap to prevent bending and damage while hoisting the rotor into 
the Derek form the ground. 
4. Run sucker rod string into the wellbore, tighten the rods according to the 
manufacturers specifications and recommended rod displacement card, 
these cards insure that optimum displacement is achieved according to 
the rod manufacturing specifications. (If f ield rod centralizers are used, 
these should attach to the sucker rods in the appropriate locations).    
5. Once the bottom of the rotor is close to the top of the stator downhole, 
record the weight of the rod string prior to entering the stator. 
6. Continue to run the remaining sucker rods taking care to lower them very 
slowly to avoid damaging the stator elastomer. The rod string will normally 
rotate as the rotor enters the stator. Note that this rotation does not always 
occur especially in extremely deep wells , therefore it is recommended to 
lower the rotor slowly in order to avoid hitting the tag bar too hard. 
7. Continue to lower the rod string until the rotor comes to rest on the tag bar 
at the bottom of the stator. This bottoming out will be indicated by a 
reduction in rod weight to near zero. (it helps to have your hands on the 
rod to feel the impact as the rod string contact the tag bar)

Wellhead Pumping Assembly – Standard Components 
 
The wellhead is the assembly of components on the surface of the well that 
allows casing and tubing to be suspended in the well as well as providing a 
means of controlling the wellbore pressure.

SHEAR COUPLINGS 
 
A shear coupling is installed at the end of 
the sucker rod  and above a pump. The 
purpose of the shear coupling is in the 
event a pump is difficult to retrieve due to 
being sanded in or paraffin build up, the rod 
is able to be pulled hard enough to break 
the rod away from the pump. The sucker 
rod can then be retrieved from the hole and 
other measures can be taken to attempt to 
retrieve the pump. 
 
There are two main types o f shear 
couplings. One is used only for reciprocating rod 
pumps (RRP) and the other is used primarily for 
progressive cavity pumps (PCP). 
 
The dog bone style coupling for the RRP uses two 
couplings threaded together. The threaded connection between the 
two couplings has a reduced neck for shear capability.

CAUTION 
ENSURE SHEAR COUPLING CONNECTIONS 
ARE ONLY MADE UP USING THE WRENCH 
FLATS. USING ANY OTHER PART OF THE 
COUPLING WILL PRE -STRESS THE TOOL AND 
THERE WILL BE AN INCREASED CHANCE OF 
PREMATURE FAILURE. 
NOTE 
MAKE UP EACH COUPLING CONNECTION ONE 
AT A TIME. DO NOT ATTEMPT TO MAKE UP A 
COUPLING WITHOUT PROPERLY TORQUING 
EACH INDIVIDUAL CONNECTION. 
The jaw style coupling for either the RRP or PCP  is a two piece clutch type 
coupling and has shear points drilled in place through the main body of the 
coupling. Shear pins are installed in the holes keeping the two components 
together. Once sheared, enough of a stub remains in order to make fishing 
easier. This shear coupling must always be installed with the male pin side down.

ROD HANDLING EQUIPMENT 
 
During the operation of a Weatherford Corig™, there are many types of strings or 
rods that are used. 
 
 Polish(ed) Rod - The uppermost joint above the top of a string of 
COROD. The polished rod threads into the top of the COROD (or 
sucker rod). T he polished rod extends through a stuffing box to 
create a  hydraulic seal. The polish rod should be handled carefully 
to ensure there i s no dirt, scratches, nicks or gouges. Damage to 
the rod may destroy the hydraulic seal at the top of the wellhead. 
 
 Sucker Rod - A steel rod that is used to make up the assembly 
between the surface and downhole components of a rod pumping 
system. Sucker rods are 7 or 9m (25 or 30 ft) long and threaded at 
each end. 
 
 Pony Rods /Handling Rods – Similar to a sucker rod but 
shorter than usual. Often placed above the polish rod as a 
method of spacing the COROD string when setting the 
pump. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
 Rod Couplings – Couplings are 
threaded connections that allow more 
than one well string to be connected 
together. Ensure they are kept clean 
and free of debris or dirt. Damaging the 
threads could jeopardize any 
operations. 
 
 
 
 
 
 
  
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18 

===== PAGE 384 =====
 
 
COROD® 
Continuous R od & Well Services

384 
 
 Sinker Bars - A weighted bar used in operations  and attached to 
the pump in order to overcome the effects of wellbore pressure and 
friction.  
 
 
 
 Giraffe String – A type of handling rod made from COROD. It can 
be made in a variety of lengths. Usually longer than a sucker rod. It  
is typically installed between the shear pin and the pump. The idea 
being that if the pump is sanded in, the giraffe will be extended up 
far enough above the blockage to ease in the fishing process. 
 
 
 Polish Rod Couplings – Similar to Rod Couplings, Polis h Rod 
Couplings are threaded connections that allow more than one well 
string to be connected together. Ensure they are kept clean and 
free of debris or dirt. Damaging the threads could jeopardize any 
operations. The threads are deeper than regular rod cou plings. 
They will be stamped with “PR” to indicate that they are for polish 
rods.

APPENDIX N – Mechanical Failure Repair During 
Operation 
 
If an equipment failure occurs during a job: 
 
1. Engage the rod safety clamp 
 
2. Install a rod clamp on the rod string as per Section 6.5.2 
 
3. Assess the situation by performing a JHA 
a. If a rig down procedure is required: 
i. Cut the rod string above the rod clamp(s) while leaving 
enough exposed rod to allow the install of a rod elevator and 
additional rod clamp 
ii. Surface the top portion of the rod string as per Section 6.6 
iii. Rig out the rod guide as per Section 7.1 
iv. Rig out the injector as per Section 7.2 
 
4. Perform all necessary repairs to address the mechanical failure 
 
 
 
 
 
 
CAUTION 
The rod safety clamp is not a permanent fixture to hold the suspended rod 
string. It simply allows the operator enough time to install a rod clamp. 
 
WARNING 
The following are hazards that may be present during this procedure. Extra 
care must be taken to ensure safe work is performed: 
 Pinch points 
 Suspended loads 
 Heights 
 
NOTE 
In some cases, the operator may need to move away from the well to perform 
repairs. Secure the well before doing so. 
D001133052 (A.7) PMTD: Stewart, Jason 2025-12-18 

===== PAGE 386 =====

386 
 
Once equipment repairs have been completed: 
 
1. Install and secure a COROD bullet overtop the rod string as per Point 3 of 
Section 4.21 
2. Install a COROD clamp on the rod string approximately 12” (0.30 m) from 
the top of the rod string and install a rod elevator on the string below the 
clamp. Mount the elevator on a rig’s hook blocks and lift the string in small 
increments to ensure the clamp will hold the weight of the rod string 
 
3. Raise the rod string high enough so that the lower rod clamp (the clamp 
installed in Step 2 of the previous page) can be removed. Raise the rod 
string approximately 7’ (2.13 m) above the safety clamp and reinstall the 
rod clamp. Perform a tap test to ensure the clamp will hold 
 
4. Rig in the injector as per Section 5.12 
 
5. Rig in the rod guide as per Section 5.13 
 
6. Ensure the rod string is secure in the injector 
 
7. Remove the rod clamp installed in Step 3 
 
8. Begin tripping the rod string ou t of the well to a field welder. Reinstall the 
rod clamp at the top of the BOP stack and perform a tap test to ensure the 
clamp will hold the weight of the rod string 
 
9. Perform a splice weld as per Weatherford COROD Critical Procedure 035 
– Rod Splice Procedure 
 
10.  Once welding is complete, remove the rod clamp installed in Step 6 and 
run the remainder of the rod string into the well 
 
 
CAUTION 
Ensure the correct elevator and clamps are chosen for the situation as per 
Section 6.5 – “Using a Rod Clamp” of the MG operation manual. Incorrect 
selection may cause a slip in the rod string and potential loss of string down 
the well.
